### 1.6. Manuscript layout                                                            21

pollutants and water consumption in the South Mediterranean irrigated areas (Suárez-Rey et al., 2019) and in the Merguellil downstream plain. Further, chili- pepper is widespread in existing cropping systems, and represents about 35% of vegetable crops’ surface in Central Tunisia (CRDA, 2017; unpublished data). The global methodological approach adopted to realize the three objectives of analysis, previously described, applied to our study system is an adaptation of the Bos et al.’s framework (Bos et al., 2004) (Figure 1.11) for irrigation perfor- mance assessment. The latter enabled to conceptualize each specific objective and choose adequate methods of analysis to use. The framework is declined into five stages based on a series of questions. The first stage, the definition of the purposes and strategies, looks at the broad scope of the study. The following stage is the design of the study, were the required data are defined, criteria and indicators are selected. Then the planned program is implemented with data being collected, analyzed and presented. The fourth stage is to act on the infor- mation provided by the analyses, with a variety of actions possible, including inter-comparison of systems or the proposition of pathways to improve the sys- tems. The final stage is to assess whether further investigations are required. This framework has been applied to each specific objective independently. The choice of the approaches and tools hence depended on the specific objectives and the data to be collected for each one. Multiple approaches and tools were used depending on the specific objective, including typology; qualitative and quantitative assessment through interviews, field monitoring and observation; as well as modelling. Analyses focused on farmers’ management rules, how these affect the bio- physical processes and resulting productivity. The description of management rules involved in chili-pepper crop production was realized through a typol- ogy analysis. This analysis provided information on the types of management rules and integration of agroecological practices within chili-pepper cropping systems. A thorough field monitoring of fertigation strategies was then under- taken considering these strategies as one of the most important impacting pro- ductivity. This enabled to collect data to model and assess farmers’ fertigation strategies using Optirrig, a crop model dedicated to fertigation context. Finally, we explored pathways of improvement of farmers’ fertigation practices by test- ing different scenarios. Detailed methodologies are given in each chapter. [SOURCE: Page_56]

In order for them to form coherent ensembles, objectives as described in sec- tion 1.3 are dispatched within these chapters. The description of the diversity of farming practices and the qualitative assessment of their potential environmen- tal impacts is treated in Chapter 2. Chapter 3 presents the field data collected to perform the quantitative assessment of the environmental impacts step, which is then treated in Chapter 4. The analysis of improvement pathways is imple- mented in both Chapters 4 and 5. [SOURCE: Page_58]

Chapter 2: Challenging agroecology through the characterization of farm- ing practices’ diversity in Mediterranean irrigated areas The purpose of this chapter was to i) describe the possible diversity of TMRs and identify, within these, the existence of environmental-friendly ones and agroecology-compatible practices or sets of practices. Additional investigations were performed to determine the types of farmers implementing these TMRs. A series of surveys was undertaken on 45 chili-pepper plots, and data related to the corresponding farm structure were collected. The sampling was carried out to cover a wide range of farm types, socio-economic and pedo-climatic condi- tions. Statistical analyses were realized to formalize a typology of management rules. Structural data at the farm scale allowed to partly determine the main characteristics of farms and of farmers implementing each type of TMR. The po- tential environmental impact of each type was assessed through a qualitative assessment approach used in ecological sciences. This chapter is written in the form of an original paper and submitted to the European Journal of Agronomy. [SOURCE: Page_58]

Chapter 3: Field monitoring This chapter describes the field data collected to quantitatively assess the TMRs. It presents a set of crop management data collected in an uncontrolled environ- ment. Eleven fields, selected based on the results of the previous analyses were monitored. Data related to irrigation and fertilization (dates, amounts), yields, etc. were collected. Field data are particularly inaccurate. Thus, the uncertainty related to each type of data was described and quantified. Then, a first analysis of yields and some crop management data was realized. [SOURCE: Page_58]

Chapter 4: Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optir- rig model This chapter analyses the relevance of a modelling approach to assess farmers’ practices based on data collected in real conditions (poorly-gauged fields). A methodological approach is proposed to perform such an analysis. Then, we as- sessed the agro-environmental impacts of farmers’ fertigation practices through [SOURCE: Page_58]

Chapter 5: Sustainability of drip irrigation systems: toward an improve- ment through agroecological practices This chapter aims at analyzing further optimization strategies through an hypo- thetical implementation of agroecological practices. Chapter 2 [SOURCE: Page_61]

Challenging agroecology through the characterization of farming practices’ diversity in Mediterranean irrigated areas [SOURCE: Page_61]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_61]

Identifying locally adapted and adopted efficient practices can be a step to- wards the agroecological transition of irrigated plains of the Southern Mediter- ranean region. However, these types of practices - often little known - are drowned within a wide range of practices. The objective of this study was, first, to elab- orate a method to describe this diversity in a semi-arid irrigated landscape, at both the plot and farm scales; second, to show the main characteristics of these types of crop management; and third, to question their sustainability ac- cording to agroecological principles. To do so, this paper focused on Southern Mediterranean irrigated cropping systems as i) studies on management routes are scarce with regard to this region; ii) irrigated landscapes are known for their high level of natural, technical and financial constraints, often favoring more intensive farming practices regarding chemical inputs. A series of semi- directive interviews were conducted in order to assess farmers’ management of chili-pepper-based (Capsicum annuum) cropping systems, a widespread crop in the Merguellil plain, Central Tunisia. The "Typ-iti” method, combining mul- tivariate analysis, clustering and association rules, was used to characterize the diversity of technical management routes (TMR) of these systems. The environ- mental sustainability was qualitatively accessed by classifying the clusters of TMRs obtained according to their potential impact on natural resources. Then, these management routes were qualitatively associated to some farm structural [SOURCE: Page_61]

Chapter 2. Challenging agroecology through the characterization of farming 26 practices’ diversity in Mediterranean irrigated areas [SOURCE: Page_62]

indicators, to analyze their diversity at the farm level. These enabled to distin- guish a gradient of farming practices and characteristics, respectively at both plot and farm scales relative to environmental impacts. The study showed that some environmental-friendly TMRs coexisted with conventional TMRs for chili- pepper-based cropping systems. Our method characterized three main groups: i) a group of intensive TMRs regarding chemical input powered especially by tenant farmers, ii) an intermediary group iii) a moderate group powered by land owners. Throughout these main groups, seven types of TMRs were described. These were always comprised of both agroecology and non-agroecology-compatible technical operations. This coexistence of diversely impacting practices chal- lenges agroecology in multiple ways. Fertilization management appeared as a major issue in the study zone, often resulting in high applied doses. These findings could allow actors in charge of agriculture to better focus their action. However, the study did not take into account the outputs of the studied sys- tems, so that their productivity and environmental impacts have not yet been assessed in a quantitative manner. For future studies, the paragons obtained from our analysis are typical cases that could be studied for such a purpose. [SOURCE: Page_62]

Key words: cropping systems; environmental impact; irrigation; Merguellil plain; Tunisia; typology. [SOURCE: Page_62]

2.1     Introduction The Southern Mediterranean region is vulnerable to climatic hazards and at a high risk of severe degradation of both biodiversity and agricultural production, due to the combination of climate change and conventional farming practices (Migliorini et al., 2018; MedECC, 2020). New patterns of action would be re- quired to address these issues simultaneously (Caron et al., 2014), with an acute need to implement new agricultural models combining biodiversity conserva- tion, climate change adaptation and food production. Agroecology, which is the production of significant amounts of food, valorizing ecological processes and ecosystem services and integrating them as essential factors in the improvement of practices (Gliessman, 1990; Wezel et al., 2014; Altieri et al., 2015), with its three pillars i) efficiency increase, ii) substitution of inputs, iii) system redesign or di- versification (Hill, 1998; Wezel et al., 2014; Duru et al., 2015; Ci and Ma, 2016), could be a pathway for a more sustainable agriculture in the Southern Mediter- ranean region (Horlings and Marsden, 2011). Agroecology suggests that actions to achieve this goal must be adapted to each agrosystem and take into account local conditions. Although recent literature is appearing on the subject (for example, de Lattre- Gasquet et al. (2017) and Ameur et al. (2020), little is still known on the potential [SOURCE: Page_62]

### 2.1. Introduction                                                                27

of agroecological concepts to address these issues in this region of the world, in particular within irrigated areas. In the North African irrigated areas, agri- culture is strongly based on the use of external productive factors (especially chemical inputs). Efficiency increases and input substitutions could appear as a first step of action towards a more sustainable agriculture. This can start by a widespread adoption of local efficient and environmental-friendly farming prac- tices. In this paper, it is hypothesized that strong social, economic and environ- mental constraints in irrigated areas (Jamin et al., 2011) might enable a diver- sity of farming practices. Could more environmental-friendly farming practices arise from this local diversity? In what extent are these practices already present or how can they be developed in Mediterranean irrigated systems? In a first step to answer these questions, the objective of this study was to i) describe the pos- sible diversity of farming practices in a typical North African irrigated area and ii) identify the existence of environmental-friendly management routes, and the types of farmers implementing them. The paper seeks to provide a complete and comprehensive description of existing farming practices at the plot and farm-scale, enhancing their diversity, and putting forward in particular poten- tial agroecological practices, but does not undertake a quantitative evaluation of their agronomic or environmental impacts. Access to irrigation is a recurrent demand of smallholder farmers in North Africa, due to expected productivity and income increases, compared to rainfed systems. Progress has been made in this matter, with the development of indi- vidual pumping systems giving access to the aquifers. The number of pumping wells has indeed increased drastically in North Africa, irrigating more than 1.75 million ha in Algeria, Morocco and Tunisia (Leduc et al., 2004; Kuper et al., 2017). The Merguellil plain in Central Tunisia is a typical example. In paral- lel, public policies sought to encourage water economy and have promoted the development of modern irrigated systems, resulting in a wide adoption of drip irrigation. But through fertigation, the latter also facilitates increased applica- tions of nutrients, at a certain environmental cost due to pollutions (Burkhalter J. Philip and Gates Timothy K., 2005; Poussin et al., 2007; Laib et al., 2018). How- ever, it has been shown that the modern irrigated systems could also enable a better management of nutrients and inputs (Benouniche et al., 2014; Al-Ghobari and Dewidar, 2018; Sandhu et al., 2019). In addition, smallholder farmers such as those in the Merguellil plain, can design environmental-friendly farming practices, more adapted to their local social, economic and environmental conditions, allowing them to achieve their production goals, depending on neither chemical inputs nor pesticides (Altieri et al., 2015). To achieve this, farmers often combine different strategies as part of a usual household management scheme (Pinto-Correia et al., 2017). Thus, [SOURCE: Page_63]

Chapter 2. Challenging agroecology through the characterization of farming 28 practices’ diversity in Mediterranean irrigated areas [SOURCE: Page_64]

the farmer makes choices through diverse ways of farming. So, it is fair to ask whether diversity is one of the main characteristics of these farming practices at the territorial scale. In this paper, we elaborate a method to describe the diversity of farming practices in the Merguellil plain – a location of multiple researches, characterized by its dynamic and diverse agricultural system (Azizi et al., 2017) – at both the plot and farm scales, by analyzing local practices. This can be achieved by char- acterizing existing technical management routes (Renaud-Gentié et al., 2014; Sa- lou et al., 2017), noted TMRs, and identifying a gradient between chemically- intensive and more environmental-friendly TMRs. TMRs are the logical and ordered succession of technical operations (TO) aiming to control the environ- ment and obtain a given production (Sébillotte, 1974). So far, investigations that integrate all the TMRs steps to describe existing cropping systems in small-scale household agriculture are scarce (Renaud-Gentié et al., 2014; Czyrnek-Delêtre et al., 2018), even the more in Mediterranean irrigated areas. Multiple stud- ies characterizing farming systems and crop management practices exist (Bellon et al., 2001; Köbrich et al., 2003; Maton et al., 2005; Poussin et al., 2008; Blazy et al., 2009; Ibidhi et al., 2018). These studies used either statistical analysis or participatory and expert-based methods. These methods have limits as they i) only identify the most relevant character of each TMR; and ii) do not describe the logical links between TOs (Renaud-Gentié et al., 2014). For instance, only quantitative data on farming practices were used to describe the diversity of coffee-based agroforestry systems in Costa-Rica (Meylan et al., 2013). Another study, examining the variability of farm structure and farming practices, high- lighted both low input and intensive systems, but was limited to the analysis of water and N use, pest and crop residue management (Caballero, 2001). The paper, focused on chili-pepper (Capsicum annuum) as a cropping sys- tem model, proceeds in the following way. First, it develops the approach used to assess farmers’ practices. Then, it describes the technical operations, their interconnexions within TMRs, and their (non-)agroecological orientation and (non-)association to farm types. Finally, it discusses this diversity of farming practices in the light of co-existing irrigation, pollution and agroecology stakes, and how these conditions challenge the implementation of agroecological prac- tices in Southern Mediterranean irrigated areas. [SOURCE: Page_64]

2.2     Materials and methods 2.2.1    Study site [SOURCE: Page_64]

The study took place in the downstream plain of the Merguellil catchment in Central Tunisia (Fig. 2.1). The latter, covering over 700 km2 , is part of the large [SOURCE: Page_64]

### 2.2. Materials and methods                                                        29

and flat Kairouan alluvial plain which extends almost over 3000 km^2 (Leduc et al., 2007). The climate is semi-arid with 300 to 500 mm of total annual rain- fall. The downstream plain is almost entirely used for agricultural purposes with a combination of irrigated and, to a lesser extent, non-irrigated agriculture. Most agricultural water withdrawal, consuming 80% of the total extracted wa- ter per year (Poussin et al., 2008), takes place in this downstream zone where a very high density of drilling is observed (Massuel et al., 2017). Cropping sys- tems are characterized by a wide variety of crops, including olive groves and fruit orchards, alone or intercropped; watermelon (Citrullus lanatu) and melon (Cucumis melo), tomato (Solanum lycopersicum), chili-pepper (Capsicum an- nuum) and other winter and summer vegetables (Poussin et al., 2008; Ameur et al., 2020). Farm structures are also diverse as far as total area, level of equipment and cropping systems are concerned. The agricultural sector is very dynamic, characterized by significant changes in farms’ production structures and orien- tation during the last decade. These changes were stimulated by i) a facilitated water access for initially rainfed farms; ii) the adoption of irrigated horticul- tural crops for some farmers; iii) the reduction of annual crops, replaced by ir- rigated olive groves and fruits orchards for others (Azizi, 2018). As one of the most widespread crops, present in most of cropping systems, chili-pepper-based cropping systems were used to represent the irrigated horticultural crops in this area. [SOURCE: Page_65]

2.2.2   Methodological approach [SOURCE: Page_65]

In this paper, farming practices include the ways of farming at both plot and farm scales. The TMR (Technical Management Routes) is a succession of TOs (Technical Operations) applied to a chili-pepper plot. A TO refers to an action or a choice performed by the farmer on the plot. Agroecology-compatible TOs or practices refer to TOs compatible with agroecology. (Non)-environmental- friendly TMRs refer to TMRs compatible with agroecology or not. The global approach, allowing to finely characterize the TMRs, give indica- tions on their sustainability and links to farm characteristics, consisted in four successive steps: i) sampling and data collection through field surveys, ii) database and variables construction, including a characterization of their potential envi- ronmental impact (in a qualitative manner), iii) statistical analysis including data mining, enabling the description of different clusters of TMRs, and iv) the iden- tification of the types of farms implementing each type TMR. These steps are described here below in detail and on Figure 2.2. [SOURCE: Page_65]

### 2.2. Materials and methods                                                         31

2.2.3   Sampling and data collection [SOURCE: Page_67]

Data collection was carried out through semi-directive interviews with farmers in Spring 2018 and was conducted in three phases. First, 65 farms were chosen for their diversity, not only in their structure (crop types, farm size, land tenure), but also in their localisation throughout the Merguellil plain. A wide range of farm sizes was covered (1-75 ha). Farm selection was actor- and expert-based, or by direct observation. Second, a series of semi-directive interviews with farm- ers were conducted, in order to apprehend the organization and functioning of the farms. These interviews concerned not only crop types and successions, but also labor origin (family or temporary), land tenure (tenants, landlords, share- croppers), water supply sources (individual drilling, collective network), and crop management (seed origin, chemical fertilizers, plant protection products). This allowed a confrontation of management routes to farms structures. Third, a series of detailed interviews were conducted on the chili-pepper crop farming practices, using the TMR concept applied to both farmers’ strategic and opera- tional decisions. The strategic decisions are made at the beginning of the sea- son and concern plot configuration, types of association, choice of crop varieties and planting date, while the operational decisions concern the technical steps of transplantation, tillage, irrigation, pest management, fertilization and harvest- ing. A list of TOs related to all the steps of a TMR was pre-established through expert-knowledge and was pilot tested with a few farmers. Data acquisition on the TMRs applied to chili-pepper was carried out on 45 plots. To achieve so, a minimum of two interviews of one to two hours with each the farmer was re- quired in order to correctly understand the functioning of the cropping system. Interviews, combined with direct observations, were face-to-face and took place within the farm, with audio record and field notes. When required, additional information were obtained by phone call. [SOURCE: Page_67]

2.2.4   Database and variables construction [SOURCE: Page_67]

Collected data were transcribed into a table of TMRs, where each TO of the TMR was described by its modalities. The TOs represent the variables used for statistical analyses. The modalities of a TO are the different values it can take. Data were both quantitative and qualitative. The quantitative data were first transformed into qualitative variables, using the “natural occurring divi- sion” approach (Husson et al., 2017), based on the analysis of the histograms of the observations, in order to maintain the modalities as observed. Two to four modalities were constructed depending on TOs. To avoid redundant informa- tion and characterize the diversity of TMRs, TOs with a single common modality to all farmers were not taken into account in the analyses. [SOURCE: Page_67]

Chapter 2. Challenging agroecology through the characterization of farming 32 practices’ diversity in Mediterranean irrigated areas [SOURCE: Page_68]

2.2.5    Qualitative assessment of the potential environmental impact of farming practices [SOURCE: Page_68]

In order to assess the potential environmental sustainability of the TMRs, the modalities of each TO were qualitatively classified according to their potential impact on natural resources, using three levels: TOs with a potentially positive environmental impact (e.g. no pesticide application), TOs potentially harmful (e.g. high value of N input) and TOs with no evident positive nor negative first- order impact (e.g. planting date). TO modalities were classified relative to each other, assuming a gradient of environmental impacts rather than an absolute value. Their attribution into each level was based on described agroecological practices in the literature (Wezel et al., 2014; Ameur et al., 2020), and exper- tise. A rating was assigned to each TO: +1 for TOs with potentially positive en- vironmental impact (agroecology-compatible), -1 for potentially harmful (non- agroecology-compatible) TOs, 0 for TOs with no evident positive nor negative first-order impact. TOs were classified as harmful or not based their contribu- tion to the stages of the ESR (Efficiency increase, Substitution, Redesign) concep- tual framework, and criteria presented in Table 2.1. This scoring was adapted from an approach of scoring farming practices for a qualitative assessment of ecological impacts of cropping systems (Bohanec et al., 2008). After statistical analysis, a total rating was computed by summing up the scores of the TOs sig- nificantly linked to each cluster, giving a potential level of environmental impact of the cluster. Four degrees of impact were defined for farming practices: high (non-environmental-friendly), neutral (intermediary), medium low (moderately environmental-friendly), and low (environmental-friendly). [SOURCE: Page_68]

2.2.6    Statistical analysis using the “Typ-iti” method [SOURCE: Page_68]

The "Typ-iti” analytical method (Renaud-Gentié et al., 2014) was used to charac- terize the diversity of TMRs for pepper cultivation. Combining three different statistical tools, the former was realized through three phases: i) a multiple cor- respondence analysis (MCA) summarizing the relationship between plots and TOs’ modalities ii) an ascending hierarchical classification (AHC) enabling the grouping of TMRs into separate clusters, within which the TMRs are relatively similar, finally, iii) a data-mining analysis to generate association rules between modalities (Agrawal et al., 1993). The results of all the statistical analyses are synthetized in a table describing each type of TMR. MCA was first applied to the TO table. Then, using the resulting coordi- nates of TMRs on the MCA axis, the AHC was performed by the Ward method, to obtain the clusters. The number of clusters, first chosen on the basis of the explained inertia rate, was consolidated through the calculation of the optimal number of clusters using K-Means partitioning. The description of clusters was, [SOURCE: Page_68]

Chapter 2. Challenging agroecology through the characterization of farming 34 practices’ diversity in Mediterranean irrigated areas [SOURCE: Page_70]

of modalities within TOs. This allows to identify additional TOs significantly linked to TMRs and not shown by the previous MCA analysis, because they were not specific to groups. Rules are defined as a succession of TO modalities common to a minimum defined number of TMRs in a same cluster. They were interpreted based on three criteria: the support (S), the confident (C) and the adjusted support, noted Sa (Renaud-Gentié et al., 2014). The support is the fre- quency of occurrence of a rule. Let us consider A, a succession of k TOs (k > 0), and B another succession of n TOs (n > 0); for A and B, the support of the rule A ) B is computed as follows: S (A ) B) = P(A [ B) A minimum support Sm was calculated by dividing the number of TMRs in the cluster comprising the lower number of individuals over the total sample size. The confident of the rule A ) B is the percentage of rules containing A and B. Confidence (A ) B) = P(B|A) The adjusted support is the support relative the number of TMRs contained in a cluster. Sa (A ) B) = P(A [ B) ⇤ (totalsamplesize) ⇤ (numbero f TMRso f thecluster ) 1 A rule was considered as linked with a cluster if S Sm, C=1 and Sa 0.5. An Sa of 0.5 meant that 50% of individuals in the cluster should contain the rule. For each cluster, the four longest rules were represented and compared to MCA and AHC descriptions. In addition, the data-mining also describes clus- ters’ similarity and homogeneity, as these increase with number and length of rules. The combined MCA, AHC and data-mining analysis led to a complete de- scription of TMRs for each cluster. These typical TMRs were finally compared to the most representative individual of each cluster, called the paragon, obtained in the clustering phase, to assess in what extend they match with actually ex- isting TMRs. The statistical analyses were performed with the R software (R Core Team, 2019). MCA and data-mining were computed using the FactoMineR (Husson et al., 2020) and Arules (Hornik et al., 2005) packages, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_70]

2.2.7    Identifying farm types associated to each cluster [SOURCE: Page_70]

This final step consisted in confronting the typical TMRs to farm structural in- formation, in order to qualitatively link the respective TMRs and their potential environmental impact to farm-level characteristics. A contingency table of plots and farm types was obtained, by cross-tabulating the TMRs of each cluster with the characteristics of the farms with which they were associated. The structural data of farms were used to classify them regarding ten criteria, representing farm characteristics, typical of the case-study-site (Poussin et al., 2008; Azizi et al., 2017). These pertained to i) access to water (“individual drilling”, “collective [SOURCE: Page_70]

### 2.3. Results                                                                      35

2.3     Results Results, organized in five sections, first present the various TOs (Technical Oper- ations) found in the study region, then describe the statistical analyses for each specific phase. Stemming from this step-by-step depiction, the overall character- istics of the TMRs (Technical Management Routes) are then presented. Finally, these are analyzed in the light of associated structural farm characteristics. [SOURCE: Page_71]

2.3.1   Technical operations used for statistical analysis [SOURCE: Page_71]

Six TMR steps were identified, constituted of one to eight TOs (variables) each (Table 2.2), resulting in a total of nineteen TOs. Variety type and crop spatial dis- tribution were the strategical choices of the farmer. Tillage, irrigation, pest man- agement, fertilization and harvesting were tactical and operational choices. As far as soil preparation is concerned, farmers had the same practice of ploughing and leveling. Two choices for the varieties were possible, with on the one hand local varieties of chili-pepper, enabling seed reuse, purchased from a nursery or grown on-farm; and on the other hand, hybrid varieties exclusively purchased, yearly, from professional nurseries. In addition, different strategies of planting date and spatial arrangements were observed. Chili-pepper could be associ- ated to leguminous, watermelon or melon and olive groves. Data concerning overall water consumption, unavailable, were not taken into account. Two irri- gation strategies were however observed, with the use – or not – of water stress periods, ranging from 10 to 30 days, which « allow good root development », according to farmers. Farmers have access to a wide range of plant protection products, applied on the basis of trade names rather than active substances. As far as fertilization is concerned, nutrient supply was mainly by fertigation. Fo- liar and soil applications were also common, depending on the strategies and product. Further, the harvest was very dependent on workforce and sale prices. The latter determined the dates and color of fruit at harvest. [SOURCE: Page_71]

2.3.2   MCA and clustering [SOURCE: Page_71]

The MCA was performed on 45 TMRs, described by the 19 TOs. The total inertia was 1.947, the two first factorial maps explained 41% of total inertia. Results were interpreted on the first two factorial maps. [SOURCE: Page_71]

Chapter 2. Challenging agroecology through the characterization of farming 38 practices’ diversity in Mediterranean irrigated areas [SOURCE: Page_74]

The two first axes (Fig 2.3(a)) opposed intensive treatment and fertilization strategies, which can be described as conventional, with more moderate strate- gies. The former was closely linked to the use of hybrid varieties, high levels of fertilization and phytosanitary treatment on the left-hand side. On the right- hand side, axis 1 is linked to the use of local varieties and much more moderate input use. It also highlights weed control strategies, off-season planting strate- gies, or preferable fruit color at harvest. The absence of organic fertilization at the beginning of the cycle is expressed on axis 2. Axes 3 and 4 (Fig 2.3 (b)) express the use of ternary fertilizers and the integration of leguminous in the association. They are also associated with medium to high phytosanitary treat- ment modalities and the highest chemical nitrogen inputs. In addition, axis 4 reflects to some extent early planting strategies, and the harvesting of both red and green fruits. [SOURCE: Page_74]

### 2.3. Results                                                                     39

The AHC, resulting in seven clusters was carried out on the coordinates of the individuals on the four dimensions of the MCA and explained 66% of to- tal variability (Figure 2.4). Fifteen variables contributed to the classification, consisting essentially in: planting strategies, number of pesticide applications, weeding/hoeing, harvest regularity, N, P, K, Ca, and Mg quantities, organic fer- tigation method, choice of variety, application or not of water stress, association with trees, association with cucurbitaceae, and use of bio-stimulant. The MCA coupled with the AHC results (Table 2.3) showed an opposition between con- ventional and chemical input intensive TMRs generally applied to hybrid va- rieties (cluster 1 and 2) and organic and moderate TMRs associated with local pepper varieties (cluster 5 and 6). Clusters 3 and 4 could be assimilated to mixed strategies at an intermediary position between the systems of clusters 1 and 2 on the one hand, and the moderate and low-input systems of clusters 5 and 6 on the other hand. Indeed, cluster 3 contains strategies not driven by pesticide use and N inputs, potentially applied to hybrid varieties. N and P driven TMRs are present in cluster 4, potentially applied to local varieties. [SOURCE: Page_75]

2.3.3   Association rules and cluster [SOURCE: Page_75]

The objective of data mining, which generated the association rules, was to iden- tify additional TOs linked to TMRs and not identified at previous steps, and to assess cluster homogeneity. Minimum support Sm was set to 0.044. The results showed that the TMRs in clusters 3, 4 and 5 were much less homogeneous than clusters 1, 2, 7 and to some extent 6. Indeed, Fig. 2.5 shows a larger number of rules for clusters 1, 2, 7. Conversely, clusters 3 to 5 had a low number of associa- tions. This is supported by the maximum length of rules for clusters 3, 4 and 5, which was lower compared to the other clusters. In other words, out of the 19 operations defining a TMR, there are in cluster 5, for instance, a maximum of 7 TOs common to at least half of the TMRs constituting this cluster, compared to 13 for cluster 1 and 10 for cluster 6. Additional TOs were identified through this process. These are included in the complete description of clusters presented in the following section. [SOURCE: Page_75]

2.3.4   Description of the seven clusters of TMRs [SOURCE: Page_75]

Figure 2.6 integrates the overall analysis of the results of MCA, ACH and data- mining, and shows the most representative TMR of each cluster. It shows the TOs linked to each cluster, used to compute the rating of the degree of agroecol- ogy (Table 2.3). Cluster 1, constituted of rather homogeneous individuals, described a TMR using hybrid varieties, intensive in terms of pesticide applications but moderate in terms of chemical nitrogen fertilization (100 – 150 kg N.ha 1 ). Planting is done [SOURCE: Page_75]

off-season (July-August), with an early weeding. For this TMR, there is no water stress period in the irrigation calendar, harvesting is often done when the fruits are green. In addition, this cluster is characterized by the use of synthetic bio- stimulants and a late return of crop residues. The cluster 1 had a high potential impact (low level of agroecology with total rating inferior to 2) so, these TMRs are considered as non-environmental-friendly. Cluster 2, equally homogeneous in nature, consisted in more intensive TMRs with regard to chemical nitrogen fertilization, but moderate in terms of pesticide application. It was characterized by off-season plantation and green fruit har- vesting. Further, this cluster had a high potential environmental impact, thus these TMRs are considered as non-environmental-friendly. trees, the use of bio-stimulant, the application of organic fertilizers before plant- ing and the harvest of both red and green fruits. These TMRs were mostly asso- ciated with hybrid varieties with a relatively low number of treatments (one to four treatments). They were also characterized by significant Ca and Mg inputs. Cluster 3 was very heterogeneous, particularly with regard to nitrogen fertiliza- tion, and is the only cluster not linked to any modality of this variable. This cluster had a neutral potential impact mining that these TMRs are intermediary between non-environmental-friendly and environmental-friendly TMRs. Cluster 4 was characterized by very high chemical fertilizer inputs (150 - 300 kgN.ha 1 ; 120 - 200 kgP.ha 1 , 40 - 100 kgK.ha 1 ), and red-fruit harvesting. These TMRs were applied mainly to local varieties with a neutral potential en- vironmental impact. Thus, the TMRs in this cluster are intermediary on the aforementioned agroecological scale. Cluster 5 described systems with moderate inputs, characterized by the sup- ply of organic fertilizers by fertigation relatively low nitrogen chemical fertilizer inputs (50-100 kgN.ha 1 ) and a relatively low number of pesticide applications (<4). This cluster essentially corresponded to TMRs involved in local varieties planted in April-May. These almost always i) comprised periods of water stress in the irrigation schedule, and ii) included association of chili-pepper with cu- curbitaceae. The TMRs in cluster 5 are moderately environmental-friendly as this cluster had a medium high total rating. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Chapter 2. Challenging agroecology through the characterization of farming 42 practices’ diversity in Mediterranean irrigated areas [SOURCE: Page_78]

The TMRs in cluster 6 were very moderate in terms of chemical inputs, with no use of calcium, magnesium, potassium, nor bio-stimulant fertilizers. Chemi- cal nitrogen inputs were very low (< 50 kgN.ha 1 ), and no application of pesti- cides was observed. However, phosphorus inputs were high (120-200 kgP.ha 1 ). These TMRs were applied to local varieties with no pest management. Fruits were harvested red. They are moderately environmental-friendly. Cluster 7 presented an atypical farming practice. Indeed, it described man- agement routes that were part of a two-season growing cycle, during which the residues of the first growth season were cut to initiate a second cycle by regrowth. This cluster 7 which described the second cycle, was characterized by rare interventions of the farmer, with no hoeing nor application of bottom manure at the beginning of the growth cycle. It was also characterized by a total absence of pest management. This cluster included only two individuals and is also considered as moderately environmental-friendly. This thorough description highlights that at the territorial scale, farming practices were very diversified and contrasted. TMRs of cluster 3 and 7 were less common in the dataset. These TMRs contained very particular TOs proba- bly rare at the territorial scale. Note that there was a wide range of alternatives to TMRs of cluster 1 and 2. Further investigation of the potential environmental impact of the TMRs of each cluster suggests that despite this contrast, not one cluster can be seen as environmentally-friendly regarding all aspects of the TOs. Indeed, almost all the clusters were all a combination of of (non)-agroecology-compatible TOs (Fig. 2.6 (b)), even though some were more moderate than others. For instance, as far as chemical fertilization is concerned, farmers commonly applied high amounts of at least one N, P or K fertilizer. At the same time, all the clus- ters from 1 to 5 were characterized by the use of organic fertilizers, which is an additional supply of fertilizer. Such behavior may be explained by the fact that in most cases, farmers tend to attribute a specific role to each type of fertil- izer, then, the amounts applied are defined independently. Further, despite the non-environmental-friendly characters of clusters 1 and 2, they also included some agroecology-compatible TOs. The most frequent, olive grove agroforestry, which offers increased economic benefits, is often undertaken on rented land. This suggests that some farm structural information, such as land tenure, could be linked to specific TMRs. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Table 4 shows that this criterion characterizes the majority of farmers of all clus- ters. The same is true for grain farming and market garden crops in general. In addition, Table 2.4 distinguishes farms rather involved in environmental- friendly TMRs and those implementing non-environmental-friendly TMRs. In- deed, tenant farmers, oriented towards arboriculture, and vegetable crops of high added value, in particular cucurbitaceae (watermelon and/or melon), which consume a lot of water, were typically associated with non-environmental-friendly TMRs of clusters 1 and 2. Due to high water needs, their access to water is almost exclusively through individual drilling. The farmers in these two groups seek maximum productiv- ity and therefore tend to invest heavily in inputs, plant protection products and even land. They generally owned a piece of land to which rented plots were added. Consequently, their Useful Agricultural Area (UAA) might be relatively high, compared to other clusters. Since plot renting is often of short duration (1-2 years), they often do not have a long-term land management plan. This is rather the responsibility of the owner. However, tenants pay particular at- tention to crop history, and generally rent plots that have not been used in the previous crop year. Land owners (“direct access” category) generally adopted TMRs of cluster 3, 4, 5, or 6. They paid special attention to crop rotation. Thus, they were often very diversified in terms of crop cultivation. In most of cases, non-environmental-friendly TMRs (clusters 1 and 2) have relatively low UAA. [SOURCE: Page_80]

### 2.3. Results                                                                     45

In addition, TMRs of cluster 6 were more likely associated to farms connected to a collective water network, that further cultivate leguminous or cucurbitaceae associated with livestock. Thus, it might possible to identify farmers adopting TMRs of cluster 6. To sum up, a (non-)environmental-friendly field might be identified, regard- ing its farmer’s type of access to land. If all or most of the lands are rented and farmer has a large investment capacity, the latter might tend to have a behav- ior of cluster 1 and 2. If the farmer is a land owner, diversified in production, connected to a collective network with a relatively small UAA, he might have a behavior of cluster 6. It seems more complex to find a typical farm for the clusters 3, 4, 5 and 7, taken individually. [SOURCE: Page_81]

2.3.6   Discussion [SOURCE: Page_81]

This study set out with the aim of thoroughly describing farming practices in a semi-arid Mediterranean irrigated area under multiple constraints, hypothe- sizing that i) these constraints lead to a diversity of farming strategies and, ii) within this diversity, some interesting environmental-friendly technical man- agement rules (TMRs) adapted to these conditions could emerge. After pre- senting the advantages and limits of the methodology, the results are discussed relative to these aspects. The methodology employed allowed to describe the diversity of farming practices among a diversity of farms, and we were able to identify some typical farm characteristics linked to some farming practices. The method we used to obtain these results is robust. The statistical tools used were indeed complemen- tary and converged in most of cases. The method of typology, combining hierar- chical classification and multivariate analyses, showed the main characteristics of the clusters. The data-mining completed the analysis by highlighting addi- tional TOs (Technical Operations) linked to them and their homogeneity. The points of non-convergence could be due to the interpretation criteria. Indeed, TOs present in less than half of the individuals in a cluster, whose weight in re- lation to the total sample is relatively high, and which are described as group specific TOs by the classical method, were not retained in the interpretation of the results. As these operations are present in less than 50% of the individuals in the group, they are not taken into account in the association rules. Clusters 2 and 7 must be interpreted with caution because of their small size. A limit of the method is the long time required to do interviews. Another important point is that in the North African context, it is difficult to obtain reliable information on certain variables (yield, irrigation doses), in the absence of precise monitoring by the farmer. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Chapter 2. Challenging agroecology through the characterization of farming 46 practices’ diversity in Mediterranean irrigated areas [SOURCE: Page_82]

Further, our findings support the statement that multiple TMRs can exist to- gether in a relatively small territory, with the identification of highly contrasted clusters. It is a way to evaluate the co-existence of farming practices in an area. Our method is intended to be holistic and systemic as it integrated all the TMRs steps described by Wezel et al. (2014). Indeed, five types of management steps can be distinguished to describe a TMR: i) practices addressing crop and vari- ety choices, crop spatial and temporal arrangements and successions; ii) tillage practices; iii) fertilization practices; iv) irrigation practices; and v) weed, pest, and disease management practices (Wezel et al., 2014)). Our method also took into account the farmer’s operating logics at the farm level, and the interac- tions between various components of this system. These have not previously been described. Indeed, much work has been done on the diversity of farms (Bellon et al., 2001; Köbrich et al., 2003; Maton et al., 2005; Poussin et al., 2008; Blazy et al., 2009; Azizi, 2018; Ibidhi et al., 2018), but very little on the diversity of farming practices (Renaud-Gentié et al., 2014; Czyrnek-Delêtre et al., 2018). These results are based on reliable data obtained after in-depth discussions with farmers. To our knowledge, this study is the first to implement this method on annual crops, confirming its applicability in a wider context than previous studies focused on vineyard. The findings showed that none of the TMRs was thought in an agroecological perspective. Rather, these appear to be a combi- nation of agroecology-(non)-compatible TOs, especially concerning fertilization practices and pesticide uses. Locally, one of the issues that emerges from these findings is that farmers commonly have a problem of fertilization management. Our study highlights the necessity for public policies, to encourage transition to- ward more environmental-friendly TMRs in opposition to the current tendency. Indeed, in general, it came out of discussion during surveys that TMRs likes those of cluster 1 and 2 are considered to be the “best” and “most productive” by the majority of farmers. The implementation of alternative practices is often due to economic reasons, as these are relatively less expensive. This supports previous findings showing that setting up market gardening (Azizi, 2018) and implementing agroecology-compatible practices ((Ameur et al., 2020) are driven by the cash flow of the farms and the cost of inputs. Thus, considering the num- ber of tenant-like farm types (mostly powering TMRs likes those of cluster 1 and 2) is increasing since last decade based on additional land rentals and high added value crops (Azizi, 2018) , environmental-friendly TMRs may be much less prevalent in the future if no action is taken. Furthermore, despite the high value of N inputs in clusters 1 and 2, most values observed in this study remain inferior to the optimum values presented in previous studies. Indeed, previous findings suggest that the optimum value of N fertilization to maximize the profitability of chili-pepper cultivation in drip [SOURCE: Page_82]

### 2.3. Results                                                                        47

irrigation was 252 kgN.ha 1 (Hartz et al., 1993) and 227 kgN.ha 1 (Zhang et al., 2010). In contrast, the observed values for P application were largely superior to theoretical requirements, in most of cases. Indeed, the optimum amount of P ap- plication for chili-pepper was estimated at 40 kgP.ha 1 by Emongor and Mabe, 2012. These are however to be taken with precaution, as pedoclimatic condi- tions also influence fertilizer requirements. It is important to bear in mind that the practices related to the moderate and low input clusters are not presented as agroecological systems per se. However, they contain a lot of single inter- esting and agroecology-compatible TOs. These results allow us to reassess the paradigm tending to associate irrigated agriculture to intensification and pollu- tion. The findings provide further support for the hypothesis that smallholder farming in irrigated areas may allow the emergence of more environmental- friendly or agroecological farming practices. This study hence challenges re- search in agroecology in semi-arid irrigated landscapes in the Southern Mediter- ranean. It confirms the existence of a large range of agroecology-compatible practices at the plot level found by Ameur et al. (2020). Can these form a ba- sis for conceiving locally-adapted, adoptable and more environmental-friendly and viable TMRs? The answer is not straightforward as the interactions among TOs and their relations with the farm-scale strategies are crucial to conceive such TMRs. At least, the high diversity of agroecology-compatible practices found in the region can be a basis towards a large-scale increase of resource-use efficien- cies of existing TMRs. Thus, actions can be taken based of these practices to ini- tiate the first steps of the Efficiency-Substitution-Redesign (ESR) process (Hill, 1998) of the agroecological transition. However, combining the agroecology- compatible practices found (such as agroforestry, leguminous integration, use of organic fertilizer etc., reduced pesticide use, use of local varieties, etc.) within new ecological-based TMRs might have social, economic and even agronomic costs. For instance, agroforestry might generate addition income but reduce cul- tivated area of green market crops. The use of homemade organic fertilizers can contribute to reduce the production costs but may require increased workload. The use of local varieties most resistant to pest attacks can also reduce produc- tion but in return, can also lead to lower yields. In addition, resource-use effi- ciencies highly depend on farmers’ irrigation and fertilization strategies which have not been studied in detail. It thus remains to evaluate whether the design of ecological-based TMRs, based on the agroecology-compatible practices found, might be viable on these three axes through an agroecological assessment. More in-depth socio-economic, environmental and agronomic assessments of current and future TMRs, taking into account the whole production systems need to be undertaken. Furthermore, our results support those observed earlier by (de Lattre-Gasquet et al., 2017) describing some trends in favor of agroecological [SOURCE: Page_83]

Chapter 2. Challenging agroecology through the characterization of farming 48 practices’ diversity in Mediterranean irrigated areas [SOURCE: Page_84]

uses of land in Tunisia. The findings could allow stakeholders in charge of agriculture to better focus their action to support smallholder farming through agroecology. Indeed, with such a diversity of farming practices, it is important to differentiate and adapt the support policies. The study can also contribute to a broader field of research on regional diversity of cropping practices. The article defined the key TOs related to each TMR and the types of farms imple- menting them. On the one hand, the regional representativity of these different chili-pepper TMRs could be more easily obtained by carrying rapid interviews on these key variables on a larger sample. On the other hand, the method could also be applied to the other major regional crops. The findings are also inter- esting for further research as far as previous studies of modeling or assessment of the environmental impact of the practices in the study zone, did not take into account this diversity (Pradeleix et al., 2012; Massuel et al., 2017; Jouini et al., 2018) or only partly (Pradeleix et al., 2018). Although the study evaluated neither the agronomic nor environmental performances of the TMRs, one sim- plistic assumption could be that the less environmental-friendly TMRs mostly linked to hybrid varieties lead to higher yields. Indeed, “hybrid varieties are far more productive than local varieties” according to farmers. This statement has been confirmed by additional investigations (Chloé Morel 2018, unpublished report; Akakpo et al., unpublished data, 2019) showing that in general, yields range from less than 12 T.ha 1 for the chili-pepper local varieties to up to 70 T.ha 1 for hybrid varieties. Hence, cluster 1, 2, and 3, mostly associated to hy- brid varieties can be considered as most productive, generating in general, up to 70 T.ha 1 . Inversely, cluster 4, 5, 6 and 7 can be considered as much less pro- ductive, less than 12 T.ha 1 , as these clusters were basically associated to local varieties. However, a mix of (non)-environmental-friendly TMRs were applied to both varieties. So, in terms of agro-environmental performance of the TMRs, one can speculate that high productivity and low environmental impacts can be achieved in systems applying environmental-friendly TMRs to hybrid varieties. Indeed, the current study, based on interviews, could not precisely take into ac- count the outputs of the studied systems, in particular yields. The qualitative assessment of the impact of TMRs and the addition of scores of TOs different in nature and with potentially very different types of impact on the environ- ment are a limit of the study. To have objective and realistic conclusions, this assessment requires precise monitoring of TMRs, in particular of irrigation and fertilization strategies. The paragons are typical cases that could be studied for such a purpose. [SOURCE: Page_84]

### 2.4. Conclusion                                                                     49

2.4    Conclusion Returning to the hypothesis formulated at the beginning of this study, we can state that at the territorial scale, farming practices in the Merguelil irrigated plain are very diversified. One of the most significant findings to emerge from this study is that technical management routes of chilli-pepper crop are very con- trasted. In a qualitative manner, the findings enable to answer the main ques- tion of the study by stating that environmental-friendly technical management routes exist within the conventional agriculture known for this region. Yet, farm- ers seem to have a common problem of fertilization management and pesticide use. The study challenges research in agroecology in semi-arid irrigated land- scapes. It gives a broader hypothesis for further research to reassess paradigms tending to oppose irrigated agriculture to agroecology. Further research might investigate the cumulative environmental impact of the studied TMRs, and their relationship to yield diversity. However, this poses a methodological problem, as it requires costly monitoring often difficult to implement in real and poorly gauged farming conditions. [SOURCE: Page_85]

51 [SOURCE: Page_88]

Chapter 3 [SOURCE: Page_88]

Field monitoring [SOURCE: Page_88]

This chapter describes a series of data collected in a smaller sample size to provide more in-depth assessment of the managements rules. It provides more detailed data on the diversity of practices, concerning water use, fertil- ization, yields and aboveground biomass production. To collect the data, a sampling and data acquisition method, different from the one used in chap- ter 2, but complementary, was used for data which could not be acquired through post-harvest interviews. The data were collected in uncontrolled and poorly gauged environments. Uncontrolled field data are particularly subject to uncertainties, which need to be taken into account in the interpre- tation. The chapter proposes a methodological approach to i) collect field data involving farmers in a small-scale family agricultural area; ii) quantify the uncertainty related to each data through error measurement and error propagation; iii) analyze farmers’ fertigation strategies and resulting yields and aboveground biomass. [SOURCE: Page_88]

52                                                    Chapter 3. Field monitoring [SOURCE: Page_89]

3.1     Introduction The surveys carried out in 2018 show the probable existence of a diversity of fertigation strategies in the plain. Indeed, as shown in chapter 2, different types and amounts of fertilizers are applied by farmers. Fertilization and irrigation strategies are thought jointly as the fertilizers are mainly applied through the irrigation system (Figure 3.1). Considering each farmer designs his own man- agement rules and based on the highly contrasted management rules identified for chili-pepper-based cropping systems, one can assume that the ways of ap- plications of fertilizers might differ from one farmer to another. The absence of this specific information was one of the main limitations of the characterization of the diversity of practices in Chapter 2. Indeed, we were unable to collect re- liable quantitative data on water use, or detailed information on the fertigation strategies during the surveys. Yet, these strategies play a key role in the resulting productivities. As presented in Chapter 1, a specific objective of the thesis was to assess the farming practices in both qualitative and quantitative manners. To re- alize the quantitative assessment of the impacts of the studied systems, reliable quantitative data on water and fertilizer (nitrogen) use and yields are needed. Thus, data collection (realized in 2019) was organized on a smaller sample size (eleven fields) through field monitoring. The Merguellil downstream plain is a particular environment, where the organization of agricultural activities, so- cial rules and prescriptions can make it difficult to have access to these data. Hence, we adopted a methodological approach involving farmers and allowing to overcome a series of constraints related to real environment data collection. This chapter aims at i) thoroughly describing the data collection methods and ii) analyzing farmers’ fertigation strategies and plant responses (yields, biomass). In the following steps of the thesis (Chapter 4 and 5), these informations will enable an intercomparison through the agro-environmental assessment of the farming practices in the plain at plot scale (Chapter 4). To perform this as- sessment but also better explain the impact of farming practices and fertigation strategies and finally propose improvement pathways, crop management data presented in this chapter have been integrated in a coherent conceptual frame- work supported by the Optirrig crop model (Chapter 4 and 5). This allows to simulate a series of biophysical processes impacted by farmers’ practices. The aim of this approach was to: [SOURCE: Page_89]

• facilitate the inter-comparison of farmers’ practices; [SOURCE: Page_89]

• estimate variables that are difficult to measure at the plot level, such as drainage and nitrogen leaching; [SOURCE: Page_89]

### 3.2. Methodology                                                                53

• project the potential gains (in terms of water and nitrogen use and yields) through changes in fertigation management, to avoid wastage and pollu- tion. [SOURCE: Page_90]

The implementation of a modelling approach required detailed fertigation scheduling on each field, but also knowledge of soil parameters, plant growth stages, and aboveground biomass and yields. These data were collected on each field. Although uncontrolled environments represent difficult conditions for agricultural experimentation, on-farm research was preferred for three rea- sons. First, it is known that there are difficulties for certain achievements and proposals of agricultural research to be adopted by farmers especially in south- ern countries (Philippe, 1990; Banhoro et al., 2019). Secondly, there is an impor- tant variability in farms’ soil and socio-economic conditions. Third, there is no available data on the farmers’ practices, reproducible in controlled environment. Furthermore, we are aware of the uncertainties related to on-farm research. It is important to determine whether potential differences in responses are due to inaccurate field measurements or related to farming practices. Hence, the error related to each type of data has been quantified. This chapter is organized as follows. After describing the plot sampling pro- cedure, the experimental design and the methods used to monitor fertigation and to measure yields and biomass are presented. Thereafter, the data analysis and error estimation are described. Then, the fertigation strategies are described, followed by a cross-analysis of yields and some crop management data. And fi- nally, these results are discussed in the last part of the document. [SOURCE: Page_90]

3.2      Methodology Data collection and the analysis of fertigation practices were organized in four steps (Figure 3.2). First, the study plots were selected based on the clusters ob- tained in Chapter 2, in order to characterize a wide range of diverse practices representative of the case-study site. Second, a protocol, allowing the acquisi- tion of a large range of data from several farms, was designed. The third step consisted in identifying the sources of uncertainty and quantifying their ranges of variability. Finally, the analysis of the collected data allowed a preliminary description of fertigation practices and resulting yields. [SOURCE: Page_90]

3.2.1    Plot sampling [SOURCE: Page_90]

The sampling was carried out in order to have all the major clusters described in Chapter 2 represented. Note that, the regrowth strategies (cluster 7), which represent very specific pepper farming practices, were excluded, i) due to their [SOURCE: Page_90]

### 3.2. Methodology                                                                  55

specificity (double cropping season) and ii) this cropping system was not iden- tified in the case-study site in 2019. Three criteria were defined to choose the fields to be monitored and ranked in order of priority. First of all, the farmer’s agreement and interest in participating in the study was necessary because of the strong farmer involvement required. The second criterion was the represen- tativeness of the management rules in relation to the clusters (paragons). Third, farms with individual water sources were given priority, assuming that the col- lective networks might generate variable water flow which might increase the uncertainty in water consumption estimation, leading to a poor understanding of the fertigation-yield relationships. Furthermore, in this highly diversified en- vironment in which only a limited number of fields could be monitored, the inclusion of fields with different varieties of chili-pepper and different soil prop- erties were privileged over different sources of water. Fifteen plots were initially selected. The study was finally carried out on eleven of them. Indeed, two farm- ers finally decided to not grow chili-pepper that season and two other farm- ers experienced transplantation failures. Seven fields of hybrid pepper variety “sahraoui” and four of local variety “baklouti” were involved in the monitoring. Except for cluster 7, all clusters were represented at least by one field, if not two to three. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Figure 3.3: The study zone with the location of the monitored fields. [SOURCE: Page_92]

3.2.2     Experimental design: field and TMR characteristics [SOURCE: Page_92]

The study started in the spring of 2019 and ended in December 2019. Table 3.1 presents the main characteristics of the topsoil layer (0–20 cm) for each plot. The soil texture was heterogeneous with sandy and clay-sandy types. The av- eraged soil texture in the topsoil layer was 53.0% ± 12.9 clay, 27.7% ± 6.9 fine silt, 6.0% ± 1.9 coarse silt, 9.4% ± 11.1 fine sand and 4.8% ± 7.0 coarse sand. [SOURCE: Page_92]

56                                                  Chapter 3. Field monitoring [SOURCE: Page_93]

Other major characteristics seem quite homogeneous: all soils are slightly al- kaline, carbonated, with a low organic matter content. The management rules were entirely designed by the farmers. All the plots were independent, and no recommendations were made to farmers. As described in chapter 2, seedlings of the local chili-pepper varieties were grown on-farm; and hybrid varieties ex- clusively purchased from professional nurseries. Some farmers had the same provider. Fertigation was operated by the farmer, a family relative or some- times by a laborer. The soil was prepared a few days before transplantation. P$_2$, P$_5$, P$_6$, P$_7$, and P$_9$ received organic manure before transplantation; P$_2$, P$_4$, P$_8$ and P11 about two weeks after transplantation. No organic manure was used on P$_1$ and P10. However, P$_1$, P$_2$ and P$_5$ used homemade liquid organic ma- nure for fertigation. Further, on P$_4$, P$_7$ and P10, chili-pepper was cultivated in association with olive trees. [SOURCE: Page_93]

3.2.3   The monitoring of TMRs [SOURCE: Page_93]

Three data collection methods were combined to monitor the management rules on each field: on-farm interviews, phone interviews and direct observations. Each field was visited at least once a week. The on-farm interviews were re- alized each time a farmer was present and available. These discussions were centered on the technical operations realized during the previous days, and the projected operations of the up-coming weeks. These were not formal prepared interviews, but rather opportunities for exchange, allowing farmers to explain the way they operate, the difficulties or constraints they encountered. The ques- tions were driven by direct observations (plant growth, soil) which play a key rule. The direct observations enabled to identify some changes or some inter- ventions realized by the farmers. They also allowed to verify whether the in- terventions projected by the farmers were realized and if not, to ask why these were cancelled or postponed. The discussions also concerned the activities on the other fields of the farms. Indeed, it is quite important to analyse the moni- tored field in relation with all the components of the farm. Moreover, additional informations were collected by phone call. These phone call interviews were also realized if no direct interviews was done for a long period of time. [SOURCE: Page_93]

3.2.4   Monitoring of fertigation [SOURCE: Page_93]

Water consumption at plot scale is difficult to estimate, since the farms are not equipped with water-meters (except for P7). But these data are crucial to ana- lyze fertigation practices and assess water productivity. So, water consumption was estimated through an indirect method, consisting in measuring, in a non- disturbing manner, the water pumping rate and the duration of irrigation. This indirect method allows to estimate not only water consumption, but also, the [SOURCE: Page_93]

Table 3.3: Description of the irrigation systems Pump            Flow          Valves    Valves capacity (hp)   rate (m^3/s)             /Irrigation P$_1$    3               15.39         8         1 P$_2$    15              23            6         3 P$_3$    7               24.5          6         2 P$_4$    15              27            12        3 P$_5$    7               20.5          12        2 P$_6$    15              20            7.5       1 P$_7$    PPI             14.6          6         3 P$_8$    7               23.13         5         1 P$_9$    10              10.5          1         1 P10   5               25.25         7         1 P11   20              27.2          7         1 [SOURCE: Page_95]

Therefore, the monitoring of irrigation was realized in two steps: the esti- mation of dates and duration of irrigation and the estimation of water pumping rates. For market gardening, the farmer usually divides his fields into several irrigation units, controlled by valves. Depending on the pumping capacity (Ta- ble 3.3), each farmer irrigated one, two, or three units at a time. It was assumed that water supplies were equivalent on every irrigation unit of a same plot. To estimate the dates and duration of irrigation, a method developed by Dewandel et al. (2007) and adapted by Massuel et al. (2009), based on temperature mon- itoring, was used to record irrigation duration. It is a simple, economical and easy to implement method on a group of plots. It had the advantage to be a non-invasive method, which was important for farmers. The principle is that “during irrigation, the irrigation pipe is characterized by a steady temperature value relatively close to the ground water temperature” (Massuel et al., 2009). The method consisted in recording the temperature of the main irrigation pipe leading to the irrigation unit and identifying the different stages of irrigation. One irrigation unit by field was selected randomly and was equipped with two temperature loggers. One logger was installed on the irrigation pipe, and the other one was installed on a non-connected empty pipe, measuring air tem- perature (Figure 3.4). The Thermochron iButtons logger (model DS1921H-F5) manufactured by Dallas Semiconductor, was used for temperature record. Its measures range from 15 C to 46 with a 0.125 C resolution. The record interval [SOURCE: Page_95]

### 3.2. Methodology                                                              59

Figure 3.4: Thermochron iButton fixed on irrigation and empty pipes. [SOURCE: Page_96]

was set to five minutes, and data were collected at a one-week time period (max- imum recording period based on the above settings). All fields were monitored with this system. In the second step of the monitoring of irrigation, the pumping rate was measured on each field using an ultrasonic flowmeter. The latter, which allows the measurement of the water flow in a non-invasive manner, uses ultrasonic waves to determine the flow rate through probes (Figure 3.5). One measurement was realized on each field and was taken at the water outlet pipe of the borehole or right before the irrigation valves. Then, the flow rate of an irrigation unit was equal to the measured flow rate relative to the number of units irrigated at the time. After these two steps, water consumption at an irrigation unit was computed and consumption at the plot scale was equal to the consumption of one unit multiply by the total number of units. In parallel to these measurements, additional informations on fertigation were collected during the surveys on management rules (section 3.2.3, page 56). These surveys allowed to complete and confirm the logger’s measurements. They also made it possible to be continuously aware of the interventions per- formed by the farmer. Information on fertigation were collected on the field, and sometimes by phone call. All these measurements enabled to establish a thorough fertigation schedule for each field. [SOURCE: Page_96]

3.2.5   Crop data: yield, biomass, LAI and root depth [SOURCE: Page_96]

The marketable yield, which conditions the economic income, is the main vari- able of interest for farmers. Chili-pepper fruit harvesting started about ten to sixteen weeks after transplantation, and lasted until December and sometimes [SOURCE: Page_96]

60                                                  Chapter 3. Field monitoring [SOURCE: Page_97]

Figure 3.5: Flowmeter probes installed on an irrigation pipe. [SOURCE: Page_97]

mid-January, with a harvest every two or three weeks approximately. The har- vest was ensured by local and family labor. Farmers did not always commu- nicate the dates of harvest in advance, as it is conditioned by manpower avail- ability and market conditions. We were therefore unable to participate in all harvests of each field. Hence, three complementary methods were used to esti- mate the marketable yield (Figure 3.6). First, yields were estimated using three 25 m2 plots, randomly installed within the plot (figure 3.6(b)). The entire fruits harvested on each 25 m2 plot were collected and weighted. The second meth- ods of marketable yield estimation was, to multiply the number of crates har- vested by the average weight of the crates (communicated by farmers), which was about 12 kg. The third method was to directly collect the total weight of fruits harvested on the whole plot from farmers. To check the validity of these methods we compared some measurements and they consistently converged to approximately the same value. To estimate dry yield which is highly recom- mended to compare the performances of cropping systems, about 1kg of fruits was collected and oven-dried (63 C for 3 days). This allowed to estimate the fruit moisture content. The total dry yield (kg) was then calculated by multiply- ing the marketable yield by the percentage of fruit dry matter. Furthermore, three plants were randomly sampled after the last harvest, ex- cept for P6 and P11 (we assume no substantial changes in biomass occurred in biomass during that period of time), where two harvests occurred after the sam- pling. These plants were oven-dried by twenty-four hours after sampling, at 63 C for 3 days, to estimate dry aboveground biomass production. Further, root depth, a parameter required to run the Optirrig model, was measured. A root profile was realized on P6 (we were unable to realize this measurement in all fields) through a root count on a 5 cm grid (Figure 3.7) and [SOURCE: Page_97]

enabled to determine the maximal root depth to use in the modelling approach. It is assumed that maximum root depth was the same for all plots and for both hybrid and local variety. The profile was realized 73 days after transplanting. Leaf Area Index (LAI) measurements were also required to perform the mod- elling. Indeed, these data enable model parameter calibration. LAI was mea- sured on P$_6$ using a destructive method consisting in scanning and measuring leaf area. Plants were sampled at different growth stages (10, 20, 40, 58, 73 days after transplanting). The leaves were scanned using a scanner-printer and their areas "A" (m^2 ) were measured using the ImageJ software (Schneider et al., 2012), as shown on Figure 3.8. We considered each plant covers an elementary soil surface equal to distance d (m) between two cropping rows times the average distance e (m) between two plant in cropping row. On P$_6$, d was equal to 1.3 m on average and e equal to 0.2 m. For each date of measurement, the LAI (m^2 .m 2 ) was computed as follow: [SOURCE: Page_98]
