3.2.6   Soil data [SOURCE: Page_100]

Soil was sampled from each plot at planting date and at the end of harvesting. A composite sampling was done randomly at three soil depths (0-20 cm, 20-40 cm and 40-60 cm). The samples were used to realize a series of measurement including physico-chemical properties and residual nitrogen. Residual nitrogen samples were frozen after sampling. All the measurements were realized by the CIRAD soil analysis Laboratory (Montpellier, France). The top soil samples were also used to measure the soil field capacity and (FC) and wilting point (WP). The measurement were realized at the LISAH lab- oratory (Montpellier). Soil aggregates were saturated for 24 to 48 hours (Figure 3.9(a)) and then put under pressure respectively at 15 bars for the wilting point and 3 bars for the field capacity. After 24 hours, samples were removed, each aggregate was weighed and oven-dried at 105 C for 24 hours. Then, dry weight was measured and the water content corresponding q to each pressure was com- puted using the following relation: [SOURCE: Page_100]

Ww Dw q=                                         (3.3) Dw where Ww is the wet weight of the aggregate and Dw is the dry weight. Three to six aggregates were used for each pressure and per field (sandy soils did not have enough aggregates). The water content at field capacity and welting point for each plot was then computed as the average of the water contents of each of the aggregate. [SOURCE: Page_100]

64                                                   Chapter 3. Field monitoring [SOURCE: Page_101]

3.2.7   Data analysis, uncertainty and error propagation [SOURCE: Page_101]

Different types of data have been collected during the field monitoring. These enabled to compute a set of variables including yield (kg.ha 1 ), fertigation sched- ule (doses and dates) and aboveground biomass (kg.ha 1 ) used for the mod- elling and agro-environmental assessment steps. However, a series of interme- diary variables were computed to get these variables of interest. The uncertainty related to each variable was estimated. The method of estimation of each vari- able and the related error is described on Figure 3.10. Some assumptions were made for errors that could not be directly estimated. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Model input variables: fertigation scheduling We considered the approach used to establish irrigation schedules reliable enough to allow the detection of any irrigation. The error on the duration of one irriga- tion was set to 600 seconds, meaning 5 minutes absolute error on the detection of the beginning or the end of irrigation, linked to measurement frequency (5 minutes). There were some missing temperature records data due to missing or stolen loggers. These missing data were replaced by farmer-said data. These farmer-said information combined with previous irrigation habits allowed bet- ter detection of irrigation phases when the latter did not appear in the logger records. Indeed, a farmer who started irrigation approximately at the same time the last two or to three weeks, might tend to do the same the following week. Thus, farmers’ indications or the average duration of irrigation of the two pre- vious weeks were used to replace missing records. Farmer-said data are sources of uncertainty mainly the amounts of applied fertilizers. We defined three level of confidence depending on the feeling and how consistent declarations were compared to data of 2018 surveys: high, medium and low. The high level was associated to a 5% percent error, medium to 10% error and low to 15% error. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Model output variables: Yield and biomass Yield (kg.ha 1 ) and biomass (kg.ha 1 ) were estimated as total dry yield and biomass relative to the cultivated area. Sources of uncertainty related to yield es- timation are multiple: methods of measurements, estimation of cultivated area, farmers’ information. Uncertainty related to aboveground biomass estimation mostly came from possible spacial variability in biomass and lost biomass dur- ing harvest. It was assumed the latter was negligible. Furthermore, a discussion section is presented on the uncertainty of the col- lected data to analyze the importance of the uncertainties and their potential impacts on the results. Three levels of uncertainty (low, medium and high) were defined and affected to each measured variable, based on i) the sensibility of some variables to the uncertainty of another one, ii) the source of uncertainty [SOURCE: Page_101]

and iii) the accuracy of the measurements. The aim of this was to appreciate whether uncertainty was high or not, which data had the highest or lowest un- certainty, which results were more accurate and which might be taken with re- serve. The collected data enabled to generate the cropping calendar for each plot. To analyze farmers’ fertigation strategies and plant responses, the total amounts of water and nitrogen supplied on all fields were compared. The related global fertigation strategies were analyzed by identifying the key stages of irrigation and fertilization at a daily time frame. Then the aboveground biomass, mar- ketable yield and dry yield were compared at both plant and plot scale. Next, we performed a primarily analysis of yield regarding management practices. The irrigation and fertilization schedules, soil properties and the aboveground biomass production and leaf area index dynamic are mobilized in Chapter 4, to model the crop evapotranspiration, water drainage and nitrogen leaching. [SOURCE: Page_103]

3.3      Results: analysis of fertigation strategies and diver- sity of aboveground biomass and yields 3.3.1    The cropping calendars [SOURCE: Page_103]

Figure 3.11 presents the cropping calendar on each field showing the periods of realization of technical operations. In general, soil preparation was done a cou- ple of days before the transplantation. The earlier plots were installed in April, while the latest were installed in July. The date of transplantation depends on whether the farmer wanted to produce earlier season, season or off-season chilli- pepper fruits. The transplantation lasted two to five days depending on field size and manpower. Irrigation started on the day of transplantation, and was realized either at same moment or a few hours before. The end of irrigation depended on farmers’ strategy. In some case, the plants could be irrigated till the last harvest (P10 for instance). The fertilizers were applied at different mo- ments during the cropping cycle. Harvest started minimum two months and half for the hybrid variety, and about three months and half for the local vari- ety. The duration between the first and the last harvest depended on the date of transplantation and ranged from two months to more than five months. [SOURCE: Page_103]

3.3.2    Duration of irrigation and irrigation schedules [SOURCE: Page_103]

Recording irrigation pipes and air temperature allowed to detect irrigation phases, since air and water temperature were highly contrasted. The average ground- water temperature was about 22 C. Depending on the irrigation occurrence, it ranged from 22 to 30 C (during the summer). Indeed, the boreholes were [SOURCE: Page_103]

sometimes about 1500m far from the fields, and transport was operated with unburied pipes causing higher temperature of water in irrigation phases espe- cially during the summer. Figure 3.12 is an example of detection of irrigation phase (overlaid in grey), confirmed by the farmer showing a plateau in records. In some cases, detection was more complex (see Figure 3.13). For example, it can be more complicated to detect the right starting or ending point when air temperature was close to the groundwater temperature. These situations mostly occurred when irriga- tion was realized at night. Discussions with farmers were particularly useful in these cases to confirm the detection. Figure 3.14 shows an example of tempera- ture recorded with missing data for air temperature (in this case, the logger was stolen). Thus, air temperature was replaced by the average air temperature of all the other fields. Further, fluctuations could be observed between air and pipe temperature during non-irrigation phases or "fake" plateaus could be observed (Figure 3.14). This made complex an automatic detection of irrigation phases. Hence, the later was thus done manually, which is time consuming but more accurate. [SOURCE: Page_104]

3.3.3    Fertigation strategies [SOURCE: Page_105]

Several water-soluble fertilizers like N-P-K (20-20-20, 13-40-13), ammonium ni- trate, calcium nitrate and magnesium nitrate, etc., were used by farmers for fer- tigation. Diammonium Phosphate (DAP) was commonly used as an additional source of nitrogen, applied as a basal dose of fertilizer before transplanting or right after. The amounts of organic nitrogen (organic manure or homemade liq- uid organic fertilizer), were not taken into account in the analysis, since it was not possible to quantify them. Nutrients such as K, P, Ca, and Mg, that play an essential role in plant growth and fruit production, were not considered. Figure 3.15 presents the total amount of water and nitrogen supplied by [SOURCE: Page_105]

### 3.3. Results: analysis of fertigation strategies and diversity of aboveground 69 biomass and yields

Figure 3.14: Example of temperature time series recorded with missing data for air temperature and showing "fake" plateaus (overlaid in blue). The black line is the irrigation pipe temperature. each farmer. These amounts vary from one farmer to another. Nitrogen sup- ply ranged from 52 to 190 kgN.ha 1 . Variability was much higher for irrigation amounts, ranging from 260 to 2800mm. Note that in some cases, the higher level of fertilization was, the higher amount of irrigation water (P1, P3 and P10). Inversely, other farmers associ- ated high levels of fertilization to relatively low water supplies (P6, P9, P11, and P4, P5, P8 in some extent). There were also cases of both low N and water supply (P7), or more rarely, low N input and high amount of irrigation (P2). The large variability in water supplies can partly be explained by the unit of the measurement used by the farmer. Indeed, irrigation is quantified in terms of duration and rarely in terms of amount of water supplied. To define the du- ration, farmers take into account their pump capacity, which they consider to define the water flow. For some of them, the lower there is horse power, the less is the flow delivered, and the longer should they irrigate. This led to long irrigation duration, even when water flow discharge was high. Furthermore, the detailed fertigation schedule of each farmer is presented in figure 3.16. The farmer commonly defined key periods when irrigation was realized. Two main irrigation strategies were observed. On the one hand there was a strategy aiming at irrigating regularly, adjusting the frequency (about 3 to 7 days on average) and doses. This strategy was mainly powered by farmers of non-environmental-friendly clusters (Chapter 2) represented by P1, P6 (cluster 1), P3, P10 and P11 (cluster 2). On the other hand, there was a strategy compris- ing two irrigation phases, separated by a relatively long no-irrigation period, of [SOURCE: Page_106]

70                                                  Chapter 3. Field monitoring [SOURCE: Page_107]

Figure 3.15: Total amount of irrigation water and nitrogen supply, for all moni- tored plots, for one cropping season. [SOURCE: Page_107]

up to thirty days or slightly more. This strategy was observed on intermedi- ary cluster 3 (P4) and cluster 4 (P8) and environmental-friendly clusters 5 (P2) and 6 (P5, P7, P9). In the first irrigation phase, a small amount of water was brought to the plant, at transplantation and a couple of days after. In the sec- ond phase, as in the case of the first strategy, the frequency was adjusted by the farmer, depending on plant’s demand. The doses generally increased with the plant development stages. On a daily time frame, farmers estimated the plant water demand based on climatic conditions and a possible state of plant stress. The decision to irrigate or not, also depended on arbitration and water sharing between different crops grown simultaneously. Concerning nitrogen applications, doses were split into several applications. The doses basically depended on the type of fertilizer used. Two fertilization strategies were also observed. In a first strategy, an important amount of ni- trogen was provided with DAP at transplantation (or a few days later), then several fertigation occurred between flowering and the first harvests (P1, P2, P4, P7, P8, P10, P11). When the farmer did not use DAP, another strategy consisted in spreading nitrogen supplies between flowering and the first harvests (P3, P6, P9). Before the first harvest (and sometimes during the harvest period), nitrogen was generally provided as Ammonium Nitrate. After the first harvests, farmers typically used calcium nitrate and magnesium nitrate to support fruit produc- tion. [SOURCE: Page_107]

3.3.4   Above ground biomass and yield [SOURCE: Page_108]

This section focuses on yield and biomass in relation with variety, duration of the cropping cycle and the amounts of nitrogen and water supplied. The above- ground dry biomass at the last harvest is presented in figure 3.18(a). The total aboveground biomass ranged from 2 to 5 T.ha 1 approximately. The higher val- ues were observed on P1, P2 and P11 while P4, P7, P8 and P10. We distinguish "marketable yield" and "dry yields" (also called "yields") as two variables of interest as the first is most interesting and informative for farmer, and the later is used to study agronomic processes. The contrasted manage- ment rules described in chapter 2, compounded with the diversity of fertigation practices presented above, resulted in a high yield variability across the plain. Indeed, the marketable yield ranged from 4.8 to more than 70 T.ha 1 (Figure 3.18(b)). The median dry yield was about 2.5 T.ha 1 (Figure 3.18 (c)) and it ranged from 1 to 7 T.ha 1 . This variability in yield was observed at three levels. First, yields varied according to variety. Indeed, the highest values were ob- served for the hybrid variety (P1, P2, P3). Inversely, the local variety generally presented lowest yields. Secondly, an important variability was observed within [SOURCE: Page_108]

### 3.4. Discussion                                                                 73

varieties. Interestingly, although yields were generally higher for the hybrid va- riety, the local variety produced similar dry yields on certain plots compared to the hybrid variety. Finally, variability was observed even for the fields of a same cluster (cluster 1, 2 and 6). That diversity might have probably been confirmed for the other clusters (3, 4 and 5), if there had been more than one plot moni- tored in the sample. The most important information that came out was that TMRs did not lead to similar yields independently of clusters. [SOURCE: Page_110]

3.3.5   Crop management data and yield diversity [SOURCE: Page_110]

Figure 3.18 presents the observed yields in relation with the duration of crop- ping cycle, supplied water and nitrogen amounts and planting density. The av- erage duration of the cropping cycle was about 190 days, regardless of variety. As far as hybrid variety is concerned, a link was found between the duration of cycle and yields (and consequently, between yields and date of transplantation, since the last harvests occurred approximately in the same period). Indeed, as shown on figure 3.18 (a), yield increases with the duration of the cycle (P10, P$_6$, P11, P$_1$ and P$_3$). A possible explanation is that, beyond management rules, the duration of cropping cycle allows more harvests and hence higher cumulative yields. Sufficient water and nitrogen can be at the origin of high yields on P$_1$ and P$_3$. But that is not valid for other fields. Regarding local variety, there was not a clear link between the yields and the variables considered here. Rather, yield variability appears to be the consequence of the combined effect of differ- ent technical operations. [SOURCE: Page_110]

3.4     Discussion The major points that must be taken into account while interpreting the re- sults presented in this chapter are: the diverse nature of the studied systems and the uncertainties associated with the collected data. One of the variables which highly impacted the results was the cultivated area as most of variables are linked to it. It was calculated considering the planting density and the total number of plants transplanted on each field. The number of plants is actually known approximately. Due to different sources of uncertainty, the amount of water applied per irrigation, the total irrigation amount, the amount of nitrogen fertilizer supplied, the yields and the aboveground biomass have a high level of uncertainty. The estimation of the plot area highly impacts these variables. Data related to local variety have a relatively high level of uncertainty because of missing informations and the assumptions that were made. However, although there is a high level of uncertainty, these are not at the origin of the observed diversity. [SOURCE: Page_110]

74                                                   Chapter 3. Field monitoring [SOURCE: Page_111]

Figure 3.18: Observed yields and relevant management data: (a) number of days from planting to last harvest, (b) planting density, (c) N fertilizer applied, (d) total irrigation applied [SOURCE: Page_111]

Furthermore, inaccuracy in the fertigation calendars might come from the techniques of data collection and the spatial variability of applications in drip fertigated vegetable crops. Indeed, the applied amount of fertilizer were less precisely measured and might introduce inaccuracy in fertilization calendars. However, the TMRs monitored in 2019 were consistent compared to the data collected from the surveys of 2018 for the same plots. This shows that the ap- proaches used to collect the data were robust and the information collected reli- able. The results showed an intra-cluster diversity of yield meaning that a non- environmental-friendly TMRs, which require high economic investment do not always lead to high productivity. Inversely, farmers were able to achieve higher productivities with more moderate management rules. Finally, yield variability might be explained by the combined effect of different management practices, and potentially other factors not taken into account so far. Operations such as pest and weeds management, the use K, P, Mg, Ca and organic fertilizer are not considered. To better explain the variability and the effect of farmers’ manage- ment rules on yield, one needs an integrative conceptual framework allowing to represent farmers’ practices and the biophysical processes occurring at plant [SOURCE: Page_111]

### 3.4. Discussion                                            75

Chapter 4 [SOURCE: Page_114]

Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Farmers’ irrigation practices generally need significant changes and improve- ments to cope with agricultural water shortage, control nitrogen lixiviation and reduce yield gaps, especially in some Southern Mediterranean irrigated areas. The goals of this paper were first to reproduce and evaluate the ferti- gation strategies implemented by farmers to grow chili-pepper, in a highly- diversified irrigated area of Central Tunisia, then to propose alternative sce- narios by modelling. The modelling approach consisted in running the Op- tirrig model for hydro-agronomical calculations (e.g. water drainage, nitro- gen lixiviation, crop growth, water and nitrogen stress) fed by the manage- ment data for each plot, then by attempts at optimizing the fertigation strate- gies with objectives of increasing yields while reducing water and nitrogen losses. An appropriate design of fertigation (frequency, amounts) in coher- ence with soil properties was found relevant to both improve yields (by 1 to 4 tons/hectare, depending on the plots) and drastically reduce losses. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Chapter 4. Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation 78 practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.1    Introduction Irrigated agriculture is responsible for about 70% of total freshwater withdrawals in the world (FAO, 2015). Drip irrigation which enables fertigation (fertilization through drip irrigation system), can benefit farmers through a decrease in water consumption and irrigation workload, and higher water and nitrogen produc- tivity (Ravikumar et al., 2011). It also enables localized applications feeding plant roots and dose splitting according to plant requirements in time. How- ever, drip irrigation may also contribute to soil and natural resources pollution (Benouniche et al., 2014) especially when improperly used to deliver excess ir- rigation (as pointed out by Venot et al., 2017). Vegetable production systems, for example, are among the main sources of diffuse agricultural pollution in the Southern Mediterranean irrigated areas, as a result of intensive fertigation strategies (Suárez-Rey et al., 2019). In these regions, the adoption of drip irri- gation led to an increase of groundwater withdrawal (Massuel et al., 2017) and the paradox of increased pressure on groundwater from the use of an otherwise highly-efficient irrigation system was highlighted by Venot et al. (2017). Most farmers did not adapt their irrigation scheduling and amounts to this new tech- nology, and even extended their irrigation surfaces, in a well-known rebound effect (Berbel and Mateos, 2014). By contrast, despite the need to address agri- cultural water shortage in general (Sharma and Irmak, 2020) and improve water and nitrogen productivity through the adoption of efficient fertigation strategies (Chauhdary et al., 2020), very few studies have sought to link the optimization issue and farmers’ constraints and practices or habits (Benouniche et al., 2014; Laib et al., 2018) which is the scope this study. When properly anchored in experimental studies through model fitting, run- ning scenarios through modelling is an alternative and advanced technique to compare fertigation strategies and the associated agro-environmental variables (Chauhdary et al., 2020). It enables the estimation of water drainage and ni- trogen lixiviation which are difficult to measure outside experimental stations, especially when studies involve multiple plots or large territories. A known requirement, especially within territories where farming practices are very di- versified is that the model needs to accurately simulate the heterogeneous soil conditions and technical management routes. Uncertainty is the issue to handle before representativeness of the modelling can be claimed: when performing model-based analysis of a particular system, uncertainties in model parameters and in observed data combine and may affect the results to a large extent (Tohme et al., 2020). In deterministic approaches, the parameters are often case-specific, without any prior estimates when a system is described for the first time, and parameters are not always transposable when multiple sites are involved. In complement, Wallach et al. (2018) highlighted that obtaining the input data and [SOURCE: Page_115]

### 4.2. Materials and methods                                                       79

parameters necessary to run crop models is more difficult than evaluating their predictions. The challenge is thus to use the right model, or at least one that is tuned to the available data and able to produce the variables of interest, and to iden- tify the adequate calibration strategy, for unmeasured parameters required for model runs. There is a broad range of model calibration strategies to identify ac- ceptable sets of parameter values out of in-situ experimentations. Among these strategies, there are manual "trial-and-error" attempts or automated approaches, e.g. gradient optimization, global or generalized Bayesian approach (Mailhol et al., 2011; Mailhol et al., 2018; Suárez-Rey et al., 2019; Tohme et al., 2020). It is altogether the context (data), the objective (variables) and the technical means at the modeller’s disposal and the modeller’s background in model exploration that dictate the choice of a calibration strategy (or the choice of complementary strategies for cross-validation to handle poorly-gauged contexts). The goals of this paper are first, to describe the very diverse fertigation strategies (implemented in the same territory, leaning on the same water re- sources and affected by similar constraints) and second, to assess their agro- environmental impacts and drawbacks before testing alternative scenarios re- garding irrigation scheduling, nitrogen applications to increase yields. This analysis focuses on chili-pepper crop fertigation strategies throughout one sea- son, in the Merguellil downstream plain. As this crop is very representative of the vegetable crops grown in irrigated zones of Central Tunisia, an explicit and portable modelling procedure was built to be easily applied to other crops and contexts. This paper is organized as follows. After presenting the sites and data, the methodology and the Optirrig model used are described. Then we present the procedures chosen for model fitting and to handle uncertainties in poorly- gauged contexts for the estimation of crop yield and water and nitrogen pro- ductivities followed by optimization attempts. Next, we present the outcomes of the calibration procedure, the analysis of modelling results and optimization results drawn from alternative scenarios. The last section is a discussion that focuses on providing realistic recommendations to bridge modelling results and experimental practices. [SOURCE: Page_116]

4.2     Materials and methods 4.2.1   Study sites and data acquisition [SOURCE: Page_116]

The study took place in the Merguellil downstream plain, an almost entirely irri- gated plain in Central Tunisia. The Merguellil plain has been the subject of many studies describing the area, the local farming practices and the main stakes of [SOURCE: Page_116]

Chapter 4. Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation 80 practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_117]

regional water management (e.g. Leduc et al., 2004; Goulven et al., 2009). Chili- pepper is one of the most widespread crops, incorporated in most cropping sys- tems. Eleven chili-pepper cropping fields were monitored between April and December 2019 (Figure 4.1). The fields are not equipped for thorough monitor- ing of fertigation. Plots were chosen to represent the main types of technical management routes applied to chili-pepper in the zone, based on a previous study (Chapter 2). The farmer’s agreement and interest in participating in the study were also taken into account for field selection. The experiment was car- ried out in an uncontrolled environment, in which all management rules were entirely designed by the farmers. In accordance, the affordable monitoring and measurements were designed to describe local farming practices at best. How- ever, the challenging experimental conditions still resulted in multiple types and levels of uncertainty for each plot, and varying between plots. Overall, this is a poorly-gauged context even if data collection has been richer on some of the plots. [SOURCE: Page_117]

Figure 4.1: The Merguellil downstream plain (a) and location of the monitored plots and weather stations (b) with illustration of fertigation practices, as injec- tion of fertilizers into the drip irrigation system (c) and injection of ammonium nitrate into the main irrigation pipe (d). [SOURCE: Page_117]

Management operations, in particular fertigation strategies, were monitored on each plot from the earliest transplant date to the latest harvest date (Table [SOURCE: Page_117]

The P$_4$, P$_7$ and P10 plots comprised an extensive olive grove, for which the annual crop was planted within the inter-rows. Only the inter-row areas are considered in this study. surface, using the ImageJ software (Schneider et al., 2012). The aboveground dry biomass was measured for each plant after drying at 63 C for 72 hours. Plant- ing density ranged from 2.1 to 3.7 plants/m2. Further, four to ten harvests were realized, depending on the plots. Harvest dates were not predefined, they were decided from market conditions. It was impossible to know all harvest dates for all plots and thus three complementary methods were used as proxies to esti- mate marketable yield. First, the total weight of fruits harvested from the entire plot was measured, whenever possible. Second, three 25 m2 quadrats were ran- domly installed within the plot, in which fresh yield was measured. Third, the farmer communicated the total number of harvested crates, and yield was then estimated, knowing the average weight of the crates. All three methods led to consistent estimations. Samples of fruits were oven-dried using the same prin- ciple as for dry biomass. On average, fruit water content was about 91% for the hybrid variety and 84% for the local variety. These numbers were used for con- version marketable yield into dry yield. Climatic data (rainfall, temperature, air relative humidity and solar radiation) were measured at a local weather station (Figure 4.1(b)). A 30% decrease of daily radiation was assumed for agroforestry systems on plots P4, P7 and P10. Reference evapotranspiration (ET0) was calcu- lated using the Penman Monteith equation (Allen et al., 2011). A unique set of climatic data was finally used, as no significant differences were found between data from the two stations. Finally, soil texture on the eleven plots was hetero- geneous with clay (P1, P2, P3, P4), clay or silt-sand (P5, P8, P9) and sandy types (P6, P10, P11) (Table 4.2). All soils were slightly alkaline, carbonated, with a low organic matter content. [SOURCE: Page_119]

### 4.2. Materials and methods                                                             83

4.2.2   Methodology [SOURCE: Page_120]

The global methodological approach was adapted from a framework proposed by Cheviron et al. (2016), which suggests three successive operations to opti- mize irrigation strategies: (i) sensitivity analysis to identify the key parameters, (ii) uncertainty analysis to identify the tolerable amount of uncertainty on the key parameters and (iii) attempts at optimizing irrigation dates and doses, re- lying on the previous information. Little is said, however, on the preliminary step of model parameterization, which consists here in testing and confronting two modelling approaches, seeking cross-validation (or at least complementary indications) to address the poorly-gauged context previously exposed. [SOURCE: Page_120]

Reference-case simulation Model calibration                       and analysis [SOURCE: Page_120]

Approach A$_1$: Trial and                            Modelling farmers' Error                                        practices [SOURCE: Page_120]

Choice of most           Indicators and adequate approach           evaluation [SOURCE: Page_120]

Approach A$_2$: Numerical Analysis Optimization issue [SOURCE: Page_120]

Sensitivity analysis [SOURCE: Page_120]

Figure 4.2: Framework of the study, with successive steps of semi-automated model calibration (sketch 1), reference-case simulation and analysis (sketch 2) and tentative optimization of farmers’ practices through the formulation of al- ternative scenarios (sketch 3). [SOURCE: Page_120]

Approach A1 consisted in the calibration on one plot, in a deterministic procedure, resulting in a unique parameter set, later tested on all plots. Ap- proach A2 resorts to a multi-stage calibration based on model analysis tech- niques, which aims at identifying a series of equifinal parameter sets. Approaches A1 and A2 are different by nature (deterministic vs. statistical) and by their ob- jectives (single vs. multiple solutions) which allows fruitful comparisons. Once [SOURCE: Page_120]

Chapter 4. Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation 84 practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_121]

model calibration is achieved and deemed acceptable (sketch 1, Figure 4.2), the analysis targets the key contextual variables and indicators (here, yield, wa- ter and nitrogen wastage, or the dynamics and severity of water and nitrogen stresses) (sketch 2, Figure 4.2). The final step is a tentative formulation of alter- native scenarios for optimization purposes (here, yield gains, water and nitro- gen savings, or reduced lixiviation) with indications for in-situ implementation (sketch 3, Figure 4.2). [SOURCE: Page_121]

4.2.3    The Optirrig model [SOURCE: Page_121]

This section gives an overview of model rationale and structure. Optirrig is an agro-hydrological model based on the PILOTE-N formalism and source code (Mailhol et al., 1997, 2011; Mailhol et al., 2018), rewritten in modern language and modular structure to easily include new processes and allow the generation, analysis and optimization of irrigation scenarios (Cheviron et al., 2016, 2020). It is used in particular to simulate yield as well as water and nitrogen budgets for fertigated and drip-irrigated vegetable crops (Elamri et al., 2018; Albasha et al., 2020). It is a three-reservoir hydrological model coupled to simplified crop growth and nitrogen balance models. Its advantage is to run with a limited number of required parameters, making it a practical tool for ungauged field ap- plications (provided uncertainties are correctly handled). Figure 4.3 shows the backbone of the model, focusing on the link between model variables and omit- ting model parameters. Figure 4.3(a) describes calculations used for water and nitrogen management, where the nitrogen stress term SN is taken from Figure 4.3(b) and combined with the water stress term Sw with direct impacts on the LAI and TDM (aboveground total dry matter) variables, that propagate through the whole scheme. In Optirrig, thermal time controls the S-shaped dynamics of LAI (with a cor- recting effect due to stresses) which in turn dictates actual evapotranspiration as the response to climatic demand. The cumulative nitrogen demand by the crop is another S-shaped function of TDM while nitrogen availability and up- take by the crop depend on nitrogen concentration in soil solution and on the LAI-dependent crop transpiration, respectively. This makes LAI the key model variable for most applications. The required inputs are (i) climatic data (daily air temperature, global radiation, reference evapotranspiration, rainfall), (ii) field management data (transplant and harvest dates, fertigation strategy as dates and doses of water and nitrogen applications) and (iii) parameters describing the soil and crop (wilting point, field capacity, soil depth, base temperature, maxi- mum LAI, shape parameters of the LAI curve, maximum crop coefficient, shape parameters of the cumulated crop nitrogen demand, root growth rate). Almost all variables in figure 4.3 are simulated at a daily time-step, except the harvest [SOURCE: Page_121]

index and crop yield, that may be "anticipated" in the course of season (in the real-time mode of the model) but whose final values are only known at harvest. [SOURCE: Page_122]

4.2.4   Model calibration [SOURCE: Page_122]

Model parameters are not always transposable from a field condition to another. Although offering new facilities for numerical methods in comparison with the former PILOTE-N model, the Optirrig model has neither been used in multi-site [SOURCE: Page_122]

Chapter 4. Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation 86 practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_123]

studies nor in poorly-gauged contexts, and only classical experiment-based cal- ibrations were performed, for different crops(Mailhol et al., 1997; Elamri et al., 2018; Mailhol et al., 2018; Albasha et al., 2020). The enriched calibration proce- dure proposed here combines a "rather basic" deterministic approach (Approach A$_1$ Figure 4.2, that targets the identification of the assumed best parameter set) and a multi-stage numerical approach (Approach A$_2$ that targets the identifi- cation of acceptable parameter sets), expecting complementary indications and cross-validation from the comparison of their merits and drawbacks. Although illustrations are provided for chili-pepper grown in the Merguellil plain and the Optirrig model, general insights are also sought as the objective is to propose an easy-to-implement calibration method for handling new, poorly-gauged con- texts. As shown in figure 4.2, the succession and confrontation of approaches A$_1$ and A$_2$ is expected to allow a suitable model calibration with insights on its merits and drawbacks, also seeking recipes for implementation in new contexts. Approach A$_1$ refers to a hand-made, deterministic calibration procedure, of- ten consisting in trials-and-errors, whose outcome is the identification of the assumed best parameter set. Although not a formal guarantee that the calibra- tion attempt will be successful and endowed with a clear physical sense, three "necessary but not sufficient" conditions should be met: (i) a common quantity is present on all plots (here, chili-pepper), (ii) informative-enough indications have been recorded for at least one of the mattering model variables, on one of the plots (here, the LAI and TDM dynamics on plot P$_6$), and (iii) the modeler knows which parameters control the recorded variable (here, the shape param- eters of the LAI curve with thermal time, which in turn dictate the shape of the TDM curve). The choice here is to adapt the deterministic calibration strategy used by Laib et al. (2018) in field conditions for greenhouse horticulture. Start- ing from an initial values of parameters obtained from a parent study (Albasha et al., 2020) and from expert knowledge, a trial-and-error process is used to ad- just plant parameters and obtain the best match between model predictions and observations on plot P$_6$. Then the same plant parameters are transposed to other plots, while using plot-specific information regarding soil depth and fertigation strategy. The underlying hypothesis is somehow that "a fine-enough sampling of the key variable(s) on one of the plots is enough to catch most of the intrinsic model behavior by manual try-and-error attempts, then plot-specific adjustments are able to provide enough complementary indications". [SOURCE: Page_123]

Approach A2 advocates a multi-stage numerical calibration procedure that leans on model analysis techniques (sensitivity analysis, uncertainty analysis) to identify acceptable parameter sets, if any (as adapted from Rigosi et al., 2011; [SOURCE: Page_123]

### 4.2. Materials and methods                                                         87

Cheviron et al., 2016). Sensitivity analysis should encompass all relevant pa- rameters (here, soil and plant parameters thought to have an influence on crop development and soil and nitrogen budget, not starting from scratch regarding knowledge on the model) and target the contextually-relevant model variables (here, the final values of crop yield, total dry matter, water drainage, nitrogen lix- iviation, actual evapotranspiration and cumulative nitrogen uptake by the crop). Its outcome is the identification of the key model parameters, which are kept for the following stages while less sensitive parameters are left aside, i.e. keep the reference values issued from the A1 Approach. The following stage is uncer- tainty analysis on those key parameters: the perturbations applied on parameter values combine and propagate through the model, finally resulting in dispersion on model predictions. Within a possibly iterative process, this helps estimating which level of uncertainty is tolerable or if there should be any further effort in better determining some of the parameter values, so as to narrow the dispersion that affects model predictions. The third and final stage is to identify the accept- able parameter sets, i.e. those that yield results "close enough" to the observed data, again with the necessity to use information-rich observation data to use- fully constrain the process, either visually (considering the envelope and shape of the curves) or numerically (considering user-defined objective functions). Among multiple other possibilities, the choice here was to use the Morris method for sensitivity analysis (Morris, 1991; Campolongo et al., 2007) imple- mented through the R software (R Core Team, 2019) and one of its packages for sensitivity analysis ("Sensitivity" Iooss et al., 2020). This method performs a par- simonious screening of the parameter space, in predefined realistic parameter ranges, and stores the "elementary effects", i.e., the effects on model predictions obtained from small changes in one or several parameters at-a-time. Each pa- rameter is then attributed two sensitivity scores, µ* that measures its mean effect when varied individually and s that measures its effect when varied with at least one other parameter, i.e. the strength of correlation effects, for the tested param- eter. The Morris method is said multilocal rather than global, as its results are obtained from a limited series of well-identified and user-controlled trajectories in parameter space, and not from "blind" statistical evaluations that would hold for the entire parameter space with no further indication. A rather large set of 28 model parameters was tested (Table 4.3) in ranges of ±20% around their refer- ence values, and the final values of the contextually-relevant model output vari- ables were chosen as targets of the analysis (crop yield, aboveground biomass, cumulative actual evapotranspiration, cumulative drainage, cumulative nitrate leaching, cumulative nitrogen uptake by the crop). [SOURCE: Page_124]

Next, after the sensitivity analysis stage, the uncertainty analysis was per- formed with the GLUE method (Generalized Likelihood Uncertainty Estima- tion: Ferre et al., 2007; Yan et al., 2020), keeping only the sensitive parameters and ranging them in the same parameter ranges. The method consisted here in the generation of 105 combinations of parameter values through random pertur- bations of the reference values (using a latin hypercube sampling method), then in running the model for all combinations and finally in selecting equally plau- sible, said equifinal parameter sets. The selected sets are these with a likelihood score above a certain threshold, where the likelihood score is an appropriate [SOURCE: Page_125]

### 4.2. Materials and methods                                                    89

user-defined objective function, in which the weight of simulations close to ob- servations is all the more heavy as the error on observations is tiny. Here, the selected sets were those within the 90% probability band around the observed data. The observation data used yields (in opposition to LAI and TDM dynam- ics on Approach A$_1$). Additional calibrations have also been tried on plots P$_1$ and P$_9$, relying on final crop yield measurements only, for the sake of compar- isons. This was to investigate the effect of plot subdivision into early season local variety plots (P$_5$, P$_7$, P$_8$, P$_9$), early season hybrid plots (P$_1$, P$_2$, P$_3$, P$_4$) and off-season hybrid plots (P$_6$, P10, P11), i.e. more precise dynamics for the key model variable (LAI as a proxy for crop growth) but less information to rely upon for calibration. Overall, the underlying hypothesis in Approach A$_2$ is that "the amount of uncertainty in the system prevents making any better than iden- tifying equally plausible parameter sets, and these should be obtained from a fine-enough sampling of the key model variable(s) on one of the plots, before testing them on the other plots". [SOURCE: Page_126]

4.2.5   Reference-case simulation and performance indicators [SOURCE: Page_126]

The aim of this section is to indicate how farmers’ practices are modelled and which indicators are used for comparison and evaluation. Optirrig produces multiple daily and seasonal variables, among which the LAI and TDM dynamics to be compared with observations, whenever available, and the final values of the already-mentioned contextually-relevant variables (crop yield, aboveground biomass, cumulative actual evapotranspiration, cumulative drainage, cumula- tive nitrate leaching, cumulative nitrogen uptake by the crop). In addition, em- phasis is placed on the dynamics and magnitude of water and nitrogen stresses, clearly associated with the fertigation strategies put into practice and known to control crop growth, biomass production and yield. For comparison and evaluation purposes, several indicators of productivity, efficiency and gains from optimization of irrigation and fertigation strategies are proposed (Table 4.4). Within the wide range of indicators available to assess irrigation performance, we consider water productivity and application indica- tors more generic and suitable to the study context. When combined to those provided in Table 4.1, these form the set of indicators available to characterize (Table 4.1) and evaluate (Table 4.4) the fertigation strategies. [SOURCE: Page_126]

4.2.6   Alternative scenarios and optimization [SOURCE: Page_126]

This section presents the optimization step performed from a numerical point of view by exploring alternative scenarios. This step aimed at converting the analysis into recommendations for in-situ implementation. [SOURCE: Page_126]

Chapter 4. Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation 90 practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_127]

The underlying objective is to identify optimal fertigation strategies for each plot, in which neither water nor nitrogen stresses occur, while no water drainage or nitrate leaching are caused, thus a fragile compromise between sufficient and excessive applications. The challenge is thus to efficiently manage soil water re- serve (which also controls nitrogen fluxes as nitrate transfers occur in solution) and to formally describe the problem in terms of model variables and operating constraints. The simplest way to address the issue and provide a reference sce- nario is to seek irrigation scenarios (and thus the associated irrigation triggers) able to fulfill daily evaporation and transpiration demands, then to eventually adjust nitrogen inputs in a second phase. The control variable is the ratio (Ratio, -) of total available soil water (TASW, mm) on useful reserve (RU, mm) evalu- ated daily (d) on the rooting depth: [SOURCE: Page_127]

4.3     Results 4.3.1   Model calibration: approach A$_1$ (trial-and-error) [SOURCE: Page_128]

Approach A$_1$ consists first in deciding which parameters to adjust, which re- lies on the modeler’s perception of the context (Which are the dominant phys- ical processes and the targets of the analysis? What are the reliable parameters that do not need adjustments?) as well as on prior knowledge of the model it- self (Which have been the controlling, sensitive parameters in similar contexts? What is known from model derivation and encryption in algorithms?). The trial- and-error attempts targets the shape parameters of the LAI curve, the temper- ature parameters, as well as the parameters attached to plant water and nitro- gen demands, biomass and yield production (see Table 4.3). Optirrig offers the possibility to easily play with model parameters; the manual trial-and-error ad- justment lead to a satisfactory representation of observed LAI and dry biomass dynamics on plot P$_6$ (Figure 4.4), but also on the final crop yield. The simulated decrease of the LAI curve in its final stages is related to the stresses that regulate the plant growth. [SOURCE: Page_128]

4.3.2   Approach A$_2$: Numerical Analysis [SOURCE: Page_128]

The first step in the A2 Approach was sensitivity analysis. The Morris method was applied to explore the sensitivity of Optirrig to variations of the whole set of 28 parameters listed in Table 4.3, reporting the effects on the selected six contextually-relevant model output variables (crop yield, aboveground biomass, cumulative actual evapotranspiration, cumulative drainage, cumulative nitrate leaching, cumulative nitrogen uptake by the crop). The analysis showed that [SOURCE: Page_128]

Chapter 4. Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation 92 practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Figure 4.4: LAI - Leaf Area Index curve (a) and TDM – Total aboveground Dry Biomass curve (b) obtained on plot P$_6$ from the Trial-and-error calibration of Approach A$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_129]

six parameters had a significant influence on the variables of interest: these are the parameters whose names appear outside the shaded regions in the (a) to (f) plots of Figure 4.5. The maximum aboveground biomass and yield are strongly correlated, as it can be seen in Figure 4.3, yield is computed as the product of biomass times the harvest index). In accordance, both the aboveground biomass and yield are influenced by variations of the base temperature of the crop (tbase), the tempera- ture sum necessary to reach the maximum LAI value (tmaxlai) and radiation use efficiency (rue). As expected, given the cited link between yield and biomass, only yield was influenced by variations of the potential harvest index (hipot). Water-related variables (drainage and total actual evapotranspiration AET ) are also influenced by variations of tbase, tmaxlai and the maximum value of the crop coefficient (kcmax: controls the magnitude of the fluxes in response to the climatic demand). The fact that the variations of kcmax were not very influential for the final values of yield and biomass tends to indicate that water was brought in excess or at least that irrigation practices have not been thought to follow the dynamics of plant demands (to be confirmed). Otherwise, the tested increases of kcmax would have impacted the expected biomass production and yield in cases where not enough water would have been present in the system to match plant demands. Together with tmaxlai and rue, the maximal potential biomass (tdmmax) and one of the shape parameters of the curve that depicts the cumula- tive nitrogen plan demand (x2) have a significant influence on nitrogen-related variables (nitrate leaching and plant nitrogen uptake). These six sensitive pa- rameters required fine-tuning calibration, which was undertaken in the GLUE selection process. The second step in the A2 Approach is uncertainty analysis, applied to the [SOURCE: Page_129]

work for the 3 sub-group of plots cited in Section 2.4: early season local variety plots (P$_5$, P$_7$, P$_8$, P$_9$), early season hybrid plots (P$_1$, P$_2$, P$_3$, P$_4$) and off-season hybrid plots (P$_6$, P10, P11), "represented" by the P$_9$, P$_1$ and P$_6$ plots, respectively. Among the initial 105 parameter sets generated to perform the GLUE analysis, only 8 parameter sets for P$_9$, 211 for P$_1$ and 1496 for P$_6$ passed the selection cri- teria, i.e. "yielded results within the 90% probability band around the observed data". [SOURCE: Page_131]

This uncertainty band refers both to the available observation and absolute and user-defined acceptable dispersion of the predictions. Hence, a possible ex- planation for the fact that far more predictions are "accepted" on the plot that has rich observation data (LAI and TDM dynamics plus the final yield value on P6) than on the other plots (only final yield values on P1 and P9): more "ac- cepted" predictions may be related to better constrained hence less dispersed predictions (the case of P6). On the other hand, 8, 211 and even 1496 are very small in comparison to 105 and the procedure may still be considered as selec- tive. ### 4.3. Results                                                                     95

The LAI curve of P$_9$ represents a particular case of high and long stress condition not well represented by the model. Further, the uncertainty range considered for the six sensitive parameters generated a broad range of curves for P$_1$ and P$_6$. [SOURCE: Page_132]

4.3.3   Merits and drawbacks of both approaches [SOURCE: Page_132]

This section compares the ability of both approaches to produce acceptable sim- ulations of yields on all plots. The deterministic approach A$_1$ was less suitable to simulate yield in the context of poorly-gauged and highly diversified farming practices (Figure 4.7). Indeed, the adjusted r^2 of the linear regression of observed [SOURCE: Page_132]

Figure 4.7: Observed and simulated yields: (a) approach 1 and (b) approach 2. The black oblique line represents the 1:1 axis. Vertical bars represent simulations error while horizontal bars represent measurement error. [SOURCE: Page_132]

to simulated yields was 0.19 with approach 1. Yield on local variety plots was systematically overestimated? It only ranged between 1.5 and 3.5 T/ha for the other plots. Inversely, r2 was equal to 0.54 in approach 2. The two subgroups of hybrid varieties were globally well simulated, as all the estimation error bars almost cut the 1:1 axis. However, yield on P4 was largely overestimated. Con- cerning the local variety subgroup, yields were either underestimated or over- estimated. Nevertheless, the simulations appear acceptable. Considering the model was proven robust to represent integrated biophysical processes of water transfer, N balance and crop growth for a wide range of crops and conditions, it can be assumed that the representation of water and nitrogen budgets on the study plots using approach 2 are also acceptable. Drainage and nitrate leaching on each plot are compared in the next section. [SOURCE: Page_132]

Chapter 4. Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation 96 practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_133]

4.3.4     Reference case simulation and analysis [SOURCE: Page_133]

4.3.4.1   Modelling farmers’ practices: water and nitrogen budgets [SOURCE: Page_133]

This section summarizes the effects of farmers’ fertigation strategies, using the A$_2$ modelling approach. Strategies implemented by farmers often result in re- source overuse and wastage. Indeed, water and nitrogen budgets (Figure 4.8) revealed massive water drainage and nitrate leaching on almost all fields. Al- though some farmers irrigated massively and others more moderately, impor- tant amounts of water relative to applied amounts are drained out of the crop root zone. Note that similar wastage were simulated for nitrogen. Drainage ranged from around 200 mm to almost 2500 mm, representing 39% to 84% of irrigation, while nitrate leaching ranged from 100 to around 210 kgN.ha 1 . Fur- [SOURCE: Page_133]

Figure 4.8: simulated water drainage and nitrate leaching for each field. [SOURCE: Page_133]

ther, important drainage was systematically associated with important nitrate leaching. But the opposite was not always valid. Indeed, even though drainage on P3, P4, P6, P11 and even P9 were slightly inferior compared to other fields, nitrate leaching was not much less. Rather, nitrate leaching simulated on P6 for instance was among the highest while drainage was among the lowest. These results suggest that irrigation strategies basically drive nitrate leaching, result- ing in high amounts of nitrate leaching, even the more when high quantities of nitrogen are supplied (P1, P6, P8, P10 for instance). [SOURCE: Page_133]

### 4.3. Results                                                                     97

4.3.4.2   Modelling farmers’ practices: water and nitrogen stresses [SOURCE: Page_134]

In this section, the fertigation strategies are compared in terms of water and ni- trogen stresses occurrence. Simulations revealed that water supply was not ef- ficiently distributed over the time on all fields. Indeed, on all fields, crops were under water and/or nitrogen stress over a part of the cropping cycle (Figure 4.9). Local variety fields (P$_5$, P$_7$, P$_8$, and P$_9$) were among the most stressed. On these [SOURCE: Page_134]

Figure 4.9: Water and nitrogen stress occurrences throughout the cropping cycle: red bands represent nitrogen stress, blue bands represent water stress, with their resulting intersection on purple, grey bands represent crop growth period. [SOURCE: Page_134]

fields, water stress primarily occurred during the first week of cultivation. This can be explained by LDP indicator (longest dry period in the 120 first days after transplantation, Table 4.1) which describes the number of days the farmers will- ingly do not irrigate in their fertigation strategy. LDP ranged from 20 to 36 days for these fields generating relatively long periods of water stress. Water stress is followed by consistent nitrogen stress on these fields, sometimes both were concomitant as for P7. This might be related to the massive drainage and con- sequently nitrate leaching generated by the fertigation strategies as described previously. Further, although LDP was equal to 20 for P2, no water stress was simulated. This can be explained by the fact that soil water reserve (facilitated by clayey soil on that field) was sufficient to cover crop requirement. P1, P2, P3, and P4 presented the highest RU (FC-WP) of the sample. Interestingly, no water stress was simulated for P2 and P4; and it only occurred during the last days of cultivation on P1 and P3. However, as for P5, P7, P8, and P9, nitrogen stress [SOURCE: Page_134]

Chapter 4. Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation 98 practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_135]

occurred, consequence of the massive drainage. Finally, some periods of both water and nitrogen stress were simulated for P$_6$, P10, P11, often shorter (P$_6$ an P11) than in the other cases. This could be explained by most frequent irrigation and sufficient nitrogen supplies. [SOURCE: Page_135]

4.3.5     Alternative scenarios and optimization [SOURCE: Page_135]

4.3.5.1   Optimized practices and expected gains [SOURCE: Page_135]

This section summarizes the possible contributions of this study for improved plot-level fertigation strategies. The optimization was focused on irrigation scheduling (doses and dates). Figure 4.10 shows the potential yield gains and water and nitrogen savings achievable through an upgrade of current farmer’s fertigation strategies. It shows that P6 and P11 were approximately at the op- timum of the yield. However an economy was possible in terms of nitrogen use for both fields; and water use for P6. For the other cases, yields might be increased by 1 to 4 T.ha 1 on average depending on the plot. In most cases, substantial reductions in water supply can be achieved. For instance, 1000 to 2300 mm of water could be saved on P1, P2, P3 and P10 and relatively less on P4, P5, and P8. However, P7 and P9 currently irrigate less than necessary and irrigation amount should be increased by 200 to 300 mm respectively in order to achieve higher yields. Furthermore, the optimized scenario generated, by limiting drainage, enables more nitrogen available for plants limiting nitrogen. Moreover, figure 4.11 (complementary to figure 4.10) compares both farmers’ strategies and optimized ones in terms productivity and efficiency. We note that P11 and P6 (to some extent) stand out from the other plots in terms of water pro- ductivity. As shown in figure 4.11, the highest values of water productivity (WP) were obtained for these two plots. As yields were almost at maximum potential values, NUE should not be significantly improved with the optimized irrigation strategy. However, 55 and 62% of nitrogen applications could be saved respec- tively for both plots, enabling a higher NUE. Water productivity could only be improved in the case of these two fields through a reduced water consumption. As far as the other fields are concerned, although nitrogen saving are possible, upgrading only irrigation scheduling should improve both water productivity and nitrogen use efficiency. Further, with current farmers’ strategies, only 17 to 66% (WAE) of irrigation water (or less considering rainfall) is uptaken by plants. WAE should be substantially increased (by 93 to 100%) with the optimization scenario (WAE opt) which should higher yields by 12 to 86%. [SOURCE: Page_135]

### 4.3. Results                                                                   99

Figure 4.11: Indicators of productivity and resource use efficiency. Indicators with “opt” represent value computed for the optimized strategy. WP: Water Productivity; WAE: Water application efficiency; NUE: Nitrogen Use Efficiency; WS: Water saving; NS: Nitrogen saving; YG: Yield gain. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Chapter 4. Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation 100 practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_137]

4.3.5.2    Description of the optimized scenario [SOURCE: Page_137]

We notice that in general, better performances could be achieved in most cases by reducing doses per irrigation and hence total irrigation amounts and increas- ing the average irrigation frequency. Example of an optimized strategy gener- ated by one parameter set is described Table 4.5 using a set of indicators. The threshold of Ratio (filling percentage of the root zone reserve) that triggers irri- gation ranged from 55 to 75%. The optimized strategy deletes all water stress leading to almost no nitrogen stress (except 1 day for P$_5$ and P$_8$ and 3 days for P$_7$). The dry period (LDP) to observe during the growing period is much higher on P$_1$, P$_2$, P$_3$, P$_4$, and P$_5$ than in current farmers’ strategies. This is mostly re- lated to clayey soil (higher RU) on these fields. Compared to farmers’ strategies, the optimized irrigation scenario has the advantage to lower the average (AIM) and maximum (MIA) irrigation amounts for all fields, but also the total irriga- tion amount (TIA) where water was overused. It also lowers the total number of irrigation in most cases, but suggests much higher number of irrigation on sandy soils (P$_7$, P$_8$). [SOURCE: Page_137]

### 4.4. Discussion and conclusion                                                     101

and assess their agro-environmental impacts and drawbacks before testing al- ternative scenarios regarding irrigation scheduling and nitrogen applications to increase yields. Using a model such as Optirrig, whose parameters are not always transposable from a particular situation to another, requires to adapt calibration processes to such conditions. To achieve the goals of the study, we first tested the two different model calibration approaches. The findings showed that a deterministic trial-and-error calibration approach was less adequate to our case-study. Conversely, a multi-stage numerical approach that leans on model analysis techniques (sensitivity analysis, uncertainty analysis) appeared more relevant. These results are not generic meaning that another calibration ap- proach might be more adapted depending on field conditions. However, our methodology aiming to test different calibration approaches to represent farm- ers’ practices in poorly-gauged environment can be easily adjusted and adapted to other conditions. The most important finding of this study was that, in this relatively small territory where farmers lean on the same water resources and are affected by similar constraints, the fertilization strategies they currently implemented for chili-pepper cropping generally generate massive water drainage and nitrate leaching. The results also suggest that water supply is not efficiently distributed over time resulting in frequent crop water and/or nitrogen stresses. Farmers’ strategies might be affected by potential arbitrage between crops for water. In- deed, when other crops are conducted in the same period, farmers might tend to prioritize one crop to another. A possible explanation of the high amounts (de- scribed in here by a set of indicators of irrigation doses), is that, considering irri- gation can be particularly time consuming for these farmers, they might decide to provide high amounts of water relatively distant in time, instead of reduced but and more frequent applications as suggested by the optimized scenario gen- erated as pathway of improvement. A similar pattern of results was obtained by Laib et al. (2018) in greenhouse irrigated horticulture in the Algerian Sahara. Alternatively, the high amount of irrigation applied might also be explained by the fact that irrigation is quantified in terms of duration and not in terms of supplied water volume per plant by the farmers. Indeed, as noted during the surveys, to define these durations, farmers took into account their pumping ca- pacity, which they consider to define the water flow. For some of them, the lower the horse vapor is, the less the flow is delivered, and the longer should they irrigate. This led to long irrigation durations. Another explanation high- lighted by Venot et al. (2017) might be the fact that farmers did not adapt their strategies to the drip irrigation technique and continue to pump water like in furrow irrigation. Furthermore, we showed that substantial water and nitrogen savings and consequently yield gain were possible in most cases by adopting an [SOURCE: Page_138]

Chapter 4. Modelling and assessing environmental impacts of fertigation 102 practices under field conditions: a case-study of chili-pepper using the Optirrig model. [SOURCE: Page_139]

optimized irrigation strategy. Fertigation practices on a limited number of plots appeared close to optimal, meaning that no significant yield improvement was simulated, and relatively few water saving was possible. Further investigation on these strategies might be interesting to understand the factors driving them. For local agricultural and water management stakeholders, understanding and promoting such strategies adapted to farmers’ socio-economic and pedoclimatic conditions, might be a first step toward an increase of water and nutrient use ef- ficiency at the territorial scale. [SOURCE: Page_139]

103 [SOURCE: Page_141]

Chapter 5 [SOURCE: Page_141]

Towards an improvement of drip irrigated market gardening systems through agroecological practices [SOURCE: Page_141]

5.1   Introduction In Chapter 2, a high level of diversity was found concerning technical operations (TOs) and the resulting management routes for chili-pepper-based cropping sys- tems in the Merguellil plain, leading to the description of seven characteristic TMRs. Within these, interesting agroecology-compatible practices were identi- fied. These, however, were not specific to one type of TMR but rather spread within the management routes. The analysis further showed that the TMRs con- sidered as a whole could be ranged from more intensive (input-wise) to more environmental-friendly. Two limits of the analyses pertain to the fact that i) the environmental impact of the whole TMR was not quantitatively assessed, ii) an economic evaluation of these TMRs was not undertaken. Chapters 3 and 4 mon- itor and model the water and nitrogen budgets and the resulting yield of plots representative of these different clusters. The resulting agro-environmental per- formances were also very diversified. This chapter seeks to undertake an in- tegrative analysis of optimization strategies of the cropping systems concern- ing the water, nitrogen resources and yield components. As such, it explores beyond results in Chapter 4 on improved efficiencies through optimization of the irrigation scheduling and/or the integration of representative agroecologi- cal practices observed in the region in the studied crop systems. This seeks to answer the following question: can the implementation of locally widespread [SOURCE: Page_141]

Chapter 5. Towards an improvement of drip irrigated market gardening 104 systems through agroecological practices [SOURCE: Page_142]

agroecological practices improve irrigation performances, regarding productiv- ity and water and nitrogen use efficiencies? Indeed, it is known that agroeco- logical practices, such as agroforestry, can improve productivity and efficiency in irrigated systems in Northern Africa (see, among others, Temani et al. (2021)). Most scientific analyses are however undertaken in experimental conditions, in which irrigation management is adapted to the observed agrosystem, bringing near to optimal amounts. The analyses then reside in whether this water is better used in mixed cropping systems rather than monospecific systems. In practice, it has been shown that farmers in the Southern Mediterranean do not necessarily adapt irrigation management to the cropping system (Venot et al., 2017). Learn- ing processes on drip irrigation management can indeed be long, with some farmers still implementing management options adapted to surface irrigation (not frequent, long applications) rather than to drip systems (frequent, short ap- plications). Thus, should the performances’ (yield, drainage, nitrate leaching, income) improvement reside solely on improved management and/or could al- ternatives to chemical fertilizers (substitution solutions) and/or a (partial) re- design of the cropping system be part of the solution? For instance, organic fertilization (through the use of manure tea (MT)) and crop associations (such as olive trees - vegetables agroforestry) are two agroecological management prac- tices that enable production with fewer external inputs or limited environmental impacts (Altieri, 2002) and have the potential to optimize land and water uses and productivity in horticultural production (Álvarez-Solís et al., 2016). These practices are widely adopted in the Merguellil downstream plain as shown by (Ameur et al., 2020). But their real impact on the productivity and profitability within drip irrigation systems and their environmental sustainability are little known. As such, for the objective of this final chapter, we propose to test the agronomic, environmental and economic interests of the implementation of a set of practices (optimized irrigation strategy, manure tea and agroforestry) to improve drip irrigation performance. This exploratory analysis is based on the data collected on the eleven fields involved in the previous chapter (3 and 4). These are referred to, using the same naming (P$_1$ to P11). The analysis is under- taken on the basis of a comparison of scenarios using a set of hypotheses. Note that the scenarios were not thoroughly tested experimentally but the analysis opens perspectives for further researches. [SOURCE: Page_142]

5.2   Practices to improve the performances of the studied systems In chapter 4, we found that the performances could be substantially improved through optimized fertigation strategies. To test whether further improvements [SOURCE: Page_142]

### 5.2. Practices to improve the performances of the studied systems                         105

could take place through the implementation of agroecological practices, we es- timate the potential effects of an optimized irrigation strategy, the use of manure tea and agroforestry on several components of agri-environmental and socio- economic dimensions (described in the next section). Numerous agroecologi- cal practices have been identified in the Merguellil downstream plain (Ameur et al., 2020): relay intercropping, agroforestry, diversification, livestock integra- tion, use of manure tea, integration of legumes in crop successions, etc. The use of manure tea and agroforestry (Figure 5.1) were chosen as these are widely implemented in chili-pepper cropping systems and are receiving increasing in- terest from farmers. This choice is also justified by the fact that the adoption of these two practices is mainly facilitated by drip irrigation technology. They appear promising, provide a lot of services as described below and lend well to assess the complementarity between drip irrigation and agroecological prac- tices. During the 2019 field monitoring, MT was used on three fields out of the eleven monitored (P$_1$, P$_2$, P$_5$). Three other plots were agroforestry systems (P$_4$, P$_7$, P10). Their were no use of MT nor agroforestry systems on the other fields (P$_3$, P$_6$, P$_8$, P$_9$, P11). We investigated the use of MT during the field surveys. In addition, an experimental design was set up on a separate field 1 (not included in the eleven sampled fields) to monitor the water sharing between olive trees and chili-pepper. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Figure 5.1: Some agroecology-compatible practices in Tunisia (Ameur et al., 2020): (a) liquid manure production basin; (b) pepper-olive grove agroforestry system [SOURCE: Page_143]

1 This field was not included in the final sample as the monitoring was not completed due to [SOURCE: Page_143]

pest attacks and massive chili-pepper plant transplantation failure [SOURCE: Page_143]

Chapter 5. Towards an improvement of drip irrigated market gardening 106 systems through agroecological practices [SOURCE: Page_144]

5.2.1   Manure tea [SOURCE: Page_144]

Manure tea (MT) organic fertilization is a well-known and adopted practice of synthetic fertilizer substitution solution in the Merguellil downstream plain. MTs, produced through an anaerobic fermentation process, are aqueous extracts produced by dipping bags of manure in water (often for a couple of days). The process is similar to compost tea production processes described by Zaccardelli et al. (2018). The latter have high contents of nutrients, humic substances and micro-organisms. Studies on these compost teas have shown multiple benefits on plants, including improved growth and yield (Siddiqui et al., 2008; Pane et al., 2014, 2015). Further, Dugué et al. (2014) note the use of MTs in the Mo- roccan context, while Ameur et al. (2020) describe its widespread use in three irrigated plains in North Africa. Specifically, these authors noted a downward trend in the use of commercial liquid organic fertilizers and the increased use of manure tea in the Merguellil downstream plain. Indeed, the production and use of MT was triggered by the arrival of commercial liquid organic fertilizers. It is a homemade solution of substitution to these expensive commercial liquid organic fertilizers. Farmers usually use about 200 L.ha 1 of MT, with a nitrate concentration of approximately 20 mg.L 1 (Leauthaud et al., unpublished data). MT is generally used in green market gardening, to produce crops such as chili- pepper, tomato, melon and watermelon. [SOURCE: Page_144]

5.2.2   Agroforestry [SOURCE: Page_144]

Agroforestry systems are also widespread in the Merguellil downstream plain, especially olive grove agroforestry systems. Even though these systems provide numerous economic and environmental benefits, there is a gap in scientific liter- ature concerning profitability and efficiency of irrigated olive grove agroforestry with vegetable crops in the Southern Mediterranean region, as mentioned by Leauthaud et al. (2021). However, Temani et al. (2021) showed these systems can improve land productivity compared to sole crops and trees, even under arid conditions. Karray et al. (2008) for their part, have described water sharing in these systems in Central Tunisia. In the Merguellil downstream plain, more than 90% of farmers had an olive grove in 2015 (Azizi, 2018). There was an in- crease of its association with fruit trees, vegetable crops or cereals between 2005 and 2015. Olive grove vegetables agroforestry for instance increased from 5% to 9% (Azizi, 2018). Commonly, farmers adopt this practice as a strategy of inten- sive cultivation to reduced input consumption. These systems also emerge from contractual arrangements between farmers with different land, water and work- force assets (Leauthaud et al., 2021). A field monitoring of soil water content was realized in 2019 in a 10m*20m agroforestry system (Figure 5.2). Soil water po- tential was monitored at three different depths (15cm, 30cm, 45cm) along two [SOURCE: Page_144]

The monitoring revealed that i) trees were susceptible to extract water along the whole transect, including at the center of the inter-row, showing that olive tree roots extended to at least 10m (Figure 5.3) and ii) due to regular soil tillage, olive tree roots were restricted to the lower part of the soil (under approximately 45 cm in depth), while annual crop roots were mainly concentrated within the upper 45cm of the soil. Hence, no evident interactions existed between the roots of these two spaces. [SOURCE: Page_145]
