Chapter 5. Towards an improvement of drip irrigated market gardening 108 systems through agroecological practices [SOURCE: Page_146]

5.2.3     Irrigation management [SOURCE: Page_146]

Farmers’ irrigation management practices were described in Chapter 3 (Section 3.3.3) and Chapter 4. As a reminder, two strategies were identified. On the one hand there were strategies aiming to regularly provide water to plants. On the other hand, there was strategies comprising two irrigation phases, separated by a relatively long dry period, of up to thirty days. Both strategies generated mas- sive drainage and nitrate leaching as simulated in Chapter 4. Thus, we do not distinguish these strategies in this analysis, and each farmer’s irrigation prac- tice is referred to as “current strategies”. The optimized irrigation scheduling generated for each field in chapter 4 to limit drainage are referred to as “opti- mized irrigation strategy”. Both “current strategies” and “optimized irrigation strategies” were described in the previous chapter through a set of indicators. [SOURCE: Page_146]

5.3      Scenario hypotheses and assumptions 5.3.1     Scenarios [SOURCE: Page_146]

Four scenarios of management are tested and include three optimized practices: optimized irrigation scheduling, the use of manure tea (MT) within olive grove- chili chili-pepper agroforestry systems, and the combination of all these. For each scenario we compute a set of variables related to the agri-environmental and socioeconomic dimensions, represented by water and nitrogen budgets (in- puts, drainage and nitrate leaching), total yield and economic data (water, en- ergy, fertilizer and labor costs, revenues and workload). The scenarios are de- scribed below. [SOURCE: Page_146]

• Non-optimized situation (NOS): Current practices. In this scenario, management rules as implemented by each farmer during the 2019 growth season and the resulting water and nitrogen budgets and yield are considered. For each field, this scenario is considered as the refer- ence to which the optimized scenarios are compared. Water and nitrogen inputs are equal to the measured amounts, simulated drainage and nitrate leaching presented in chapter 4. [SOURCE: Page_146]

• Scenario 1 (S1): Optimized irrigation strategy In this scenario, all technical operations remain unchanged, except irriga- tion strategies. The farmers’ strategies are replaced by the optimized ones concerning dates and doses of irrigation applied to the crop. Irrigation wa- ter supply is basically equal to chili-pepper water requirements. The dates [SOURCE: Page_146]

### 5.3. Scenario hypotheses and assumptions                                          109

and doses were generated through the optimization process implemented in chapter 4. [SOURCE: Page_147]

• Scenario 2 (S$_2$): Agroecological system In this scenario, all chili-pepper fields are considered in association with olive trees, to form an agroforestry system. The current practices of each farmer, as in current practices, are applied and where relevant, it is as- sumed that the farmer further uses manure tea, without modifying other technical operations. We assumed a same planting density for all plots (100 trees per hectare, with a spacing of 10m*10m). [SOURCE: Page_147]

• Scenario 3 (S$_3$): Water optimized agroecological system. This strategy combines the two previous scenarios: Scenario 1 and 2. It is assumed that chili-pepper is grown in an agroforestry system, in which MT is used in addition to the other technical operations originally imple- mented by the farmers. The irrigation doses and dates are those used in scenario 1 (generated in chapter 4) plus olive trees water requirement. [SOURCE: Page_147]

For each scenario, some hypotheses and assumptions were made to deter- mine the different variables (water input, drainage, nitrate leaching, yield and income and some additional socioeconomic variables). The formula used to compute these variables for each scenario are presented in Table 5.1. [SOURCE: Page_147]

5.3.2    Water and nitrogen budgets [SOURCE: Page_147]

A simplified water balance equation was used: [SOURCE: Page_147]

I+P     ET    D    dS = 0                        (5.1) [SOURCE: Page_147]

where I = Irrigation amount, P = Rainfall, ET = evapotranspiration (crop water demand), D = Drainage, dS = residual stock. To simplify calculations, dS was set to 0. We assumed that crops’ water re- quirements were solely provided by irrigation, hence P was also set to 0. In mono-species scenarios (NOS and S$_1$), ET is equal chili-pepper water demand. In agroforestry scenarios (S$_2$ and S$_4$), it is equal to both chili-pepper and olive trees water demand. Hence, for any given scenario, equation 5.1 becomes: [SOURCE: Page_147]

where ETP = chili-pepper water demand and ETO = olive trees water demand (if applicable; if not, ETO = 0). Olive tree water demand was set 600 mm (FAO, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_147]

Chapter 5. Towards an improvement of drip irrigated market gardening 110 systems through agroecological practices [SOURCE: Page_148]

The functioning of agroforestry systems (Scenarios 3 and 4) is hard to repre- sent due to the large number of interactions taking place between species con- cerning light, water and nitrogen uses. Both competition and facilitation interac- tions can exist depending on conditions (He et al., 2013). In hot conditions, trees can improve crops’ growing conditions by providing shade. But the decrease in received radiation under olive trees for annual crop, can be limiting for evapo- transpiration in other conditions. We did not analyze all the interactions within agroforestry systems for this study. Based on monitoring described in section 5.2.2, water and nitrogen sharing principle in agroforestry systems is simplified as follow: [SOURCE: Page_148]

• no interactions exist between the two species root systems and water and nutrient are primarily supplied to chili-pepper. [SOURCE: Page_148]

• olive trees uptake water and nitrogen from deep horizons, below the chili- pepper root zone. Hence, drained water and leached nitrate from the chili- pepper root zone can be used by olive trees. [SOURCE: Page_148]

For all scenarios, nitrogen input amounts were maintained similar to NOS. To compute the nitrogen budget in agroforestry scenarios, we set the nitrogen uptake of olive trees to 45 kg.ha 1 (Fernández-Escobar, 2011) although various amounts are proposed in the literature. Nitrogen supply of MT was negligible (see section 5.2.1). [SOURCE: Page_148]

5.3.3     Yield components [SOURCE: Page_148]

The presence of trees within the agroforestry system reduces the planting area of the annual crop. Considering a typical field structure of an agroforestry system (10x^10m rows for olive trees, chili-pepper intercropped at a 1.5 m distance from trees and row distances of 1.2 m), chili-pepper yield decrease due to the direct decrease of planted area (and eventually other competition interaction, e.g. for light) was considered to be 20%2 . Olive yield was set to 50 kg per tree (5 T.ha 1 on average) (Morel 2018 unpublished report, FAO, 2021). Previous studies have shown that use of compost teas results in increased yields (Álvarez-Solís et al., 2016), approximately by 20% (Zaccardelli et al., 2018). Hence in the Scenario 3, the use of MT compensates the decrease due to crop association, leading to a 0 increase in yield compared to reference situation for chili-pepper. For farmers already using MT (P$_1$, P$_2$, P$_5$ ) or fields already in agroforestry (P$_4$, P$_7$, P10), a 20% decrease or in increase in yield was applied. To determine the interest of each scenario, we computed a ratio as follows for the 2 Value set arbitrary. Temani et al., 2021 estimated this decrease to 50% for faba been and wheat [SOURCE: Page_148]

in rain-fed conditions. [SOURCE: Page_148]

A Yield ratio > 0 suggests that production in the scenario is higher than that of the reference situation (current practices). Ratio of 0 indicates no effect and ratio < 0 indicates lower production. In agroforestry scenarios, the ratio can be interpreted as a land equivalent ratio (LER) of the annual crop, which is the ratio between its yield in an agroforestry system in comparison to its yield in a mono-culture over the same period. [SOURCE: Page_149]

5.3.4   Gross revenue and other socio-economic variables [SOURCE: Page_149]

Gross revenue was calculated by multiplying the total marketable yield har- vested by an average price of 1 TND.kg 1 for chili-pepper and 1.3 TND.kg 1 for olive (Morel 2018, unpublished report). This is a simplistic approach as prices substantially vary during the season. Further, field monitoring involved a partial acquisition of economic data and workload. The current analysis hence draws from this local knowledge – rather qualitative in nature –, completed by a set of hypotheses. Energy use and costs were variable and depended on energy sources. Labor costs also varied depending on the type of activity, while fertil- izer costs depended on the commercial form of the products used. Thus, three levels of effects of scenarios on water/energy, labor, and fertilizer costs, and working time were defined: substantial decrease (- - -), negligible effect (NE), [SOURCE: Page_149]

substantial increase (+++). A substantial decrease in input amounts generated substantial decreases in costs and vice versa. The number of days of irrigation (Table 4.5) was used to estimate the impact of irrigation on workload. We con- sidered that the farmers themselves managed irrigation activity, as observed in all the monitored fields, even though it was sometimes realized by a third party. So, irrigation and the use of MT had a negligible effect on labor costs. Additional costs existed in the case of yield increases (increasing harvesting time) and olive tree pruning. 5.4      Results In addition to the optimization through irrigation management, this chapter seeks to assess whether further improvements could be realized through the implementation of agroecological practices. The most important information resulting from the analyses is that all three improvement scenarios tested have agro-environmental and economic interests, but also limits. They can potentially contribute to better performances regarding agro-environmental variables but less interesting economically for some farmers. For other, they might enable to [SOURCE: Page_150]

### 5.4. Results                                                                    113

reduce the environmental impacts (water withdrawals, drainage, nitrate leach- ing) while increasing productivity and eventually income. In most of cases, the three scenarios were more productive and/or profitable than the current prac- tices. [SOURCE: Page_151]

5.4.1    Water and nitrogen budgets [SOURCE: Page_151]

Figures 5.4 and 5.5 represent the quantitative variables. A table of both quali- tative and quantitative variables is presented in appendix. On both figures, the variables were normalized to range between 0 and 1. The charts are grouped ac- cording to clusters (obtained in chapter 2). We note that all three scenarios might allow to reduce/limit water drainage but also nitrate leaching in all fields. In- deed, with an optimized irrigation strategy (S$_2$ or S$_4$), the required amount of water is applied, resulting in limited drainage. In agroforestry scenarios, the olive tree root system enables a recycling of resources drained from the chili- pepper root zone. However, in the cases where relatively limited amounts of water are applied by farmers in reference situation, additional irrigation is re- quired to cover both species requirements in agroforestry systems (S$_3$ and S$_4$), e.g. P$_7$ and P$_9$. We also note that the proportions of savings (water and nitro- gen) vary depending on fields, and more importantly between the fields of a same cluster (P$_1$ and P$_6$ of cluster 1 for example). [SOURCE: Page_151]

5.4.2    Yield components [SOURCE: Page_151]

As simulated in the previous chapter, S1 might generate higher productivities than reference situation in most cases. S2 might also higher yields in some cases, especially in field already in agroforestry, due to the use of MT. It should be less interesting regarding yield for P1, P2 and P5 as these already integrate the use of MT. Indeed, association with olive trees with same management rules imple- mented by these farmers substantially decreased yield due to lower cultivated area for chili-pepper (P1, P2, P5) and competition interactions. However, the "Ratio" for olive tree or the land equivalent ratio of the whole system might be higher, as far as fertigation enables “higher production of olive in agroforestry systems, compared to mono-culture” according to farmers. Hence, the loss in chili-pepper might somehow be compensated by the olive trees. Further, note that no substantial changes in yield might exist in S1, S2, and S3, if the realized yield in S1 is close to the optimum as for P6 and P11, but additional revenue could be generated true agroforestry. [SOURCE: Page_151]

### 5.4. Results                                                           115

Figure 5.5: Agro-environmental variable and socio-economic variables (P1 to P6) [SOURCE: Page_153]

Chapter 5. Towards an improvement of drip irrigated market gardening 116 systems through agroecological practices [SOURCE: Page_154]

5.4.3    Socio-economic impacts [SOURCE: Page_154]

On the economic dimension, S$_1$ might enable substantial reduction of water costs in case of over-irrigation in current practices (P$_1$, P$_2$, P$_3$ and P10). In- versely, it can generate additional costs if crops water requirements were not met. These costs would be even more significant in an agroforestry system (S$_3$) to meet olive tree demands. But actually, farmers do not take into account olive trees’ requirements to design annual crop managements rules. Unless further in- creases in yields are not possible, the scenarios we propose generate additional working time and labor cost, related to additional harvests, manure purchase or olive tree pruning. However, these costs are counter-weighed by additional income. In addition, S$_1$ and S$_3$ can be very time consuming and binding. In- deed, the number of irrigation days (see Table 4.5, Chapter 4) can double with the optimized irrigation strategy. For example, it would rise by a factor of 5 to 6, respectively for P$_9$ and P$_7$. This increase related to the optimization process was due to the need to irrigate more often on draining sandy soils. Furthermore, concerning economic data, the revenue is an interesting indi- cator to compare scenarios as well as systems, even if it is not the most relevant one. In general, the scenarios we propose generate substantial increase in rev- enues consecutive to increases in yield. However, revenue varies considerably depending on scenarios, fields and even clusters. For example, S$_1$ could almost double income of P$_1$ whereas S$_3$ could lower it by about 20%. For the same field, S$_2$ would have generated higher income than current practices but lower S$_3$. These conclusions are valid for all fields already using MT like P$_1$. In the other cases, S$_1$ gives higher income coming from increased yield. S$_2$ and S$_3$ might produce additional income from olives. To sum up, Water optimized agroecological system (S$_3$) appears as an in- teresting trade-off to at least the current production systems, by both reducing resource wastage and generating additional income for some farmers (P$_1$, P$_2$, P$_3$, P$_4$, P10). Although it can increase revenue, the use of MT combined with agroforestry (scenario 2) in the current practices offers marginal environmental interest regarding the current fertigation strategies. Environmentally, S$_1$ is the ideal alternative, but its implementation (as S$_3$) could be binding. [SOURCE: Page_154]

5.5      Discussion and conclusion This chapter sought out to answer questions of a possible improvement of fer- tigation performances, regarding productivity and water and nitrogen use effi- ciencies through the implementation of locally widespread agroecological prac- tices. The three alternative scenarios tested in the analyses revealed agro-envi- ronmental and economic interests, but also limits. We found that alternative [SOURCE: Page_154]

### 5.5. Discussion and conclusion                                                     117

scenarios can be environmentally viable but less interesting economically for some farmers and inversely for others. As described previously, there is an evident scope for improvement in most of the fields. The pathways of improvements, whether through optimized irriga- tion on agroecological practices, depend on the specific context of each plot, and must therefore be analyzed on a case-by-case basis. Hence, their is no generic scenario of improvement and each farmer’s case should be handled separately. However, as a first of action at territorial scale, the improvement of resource use efficiency and yields is primarily achievable through better management of ir- rigation, and for systems that manage their inputs (water and nitrogen) well, through the implementation of agroecological practices. Irrigation, on the other hand, generates additional work, which is not always compatible with the farm- ers’ work schedule. These conclusions must be taken with caution. The increases in yield and revenue presented are hypothetical and correspond to an actually cultivated area of one hectare. In the practice, the yield per surface might be inferior as the actually cultivated area on a field are often largely inferior to the field area. We are aware that the analyses presented in the section can appear speculative. However, the observed and hypothetical marketable yields presented tie well with observed yields. Another limitation of this work is the absence of exper- imental data to support the findings. In-depth multicultural analysis might be undertaken to confirm the conclusions. Nonetheless, based on the simplistic assumptions we made, we showed that agroecological practices are an inter- esting path to improve sustainability of drip irrigation agriculture in the Mer- guellil downstream plain. However their implementation within the current practices might not solely lead to better efficiency, productivity and profitability. Adapting fertigation strategies and combining them with agroecological prac- tices seems more promising although challenging. The analyses confirm the importance to adapt agroecological practices to local conditions and farmers’ contexts. This preliminary investigation goes somehow against previous liter- ature on the subject (Wezel, 2017) stating that agroecological practices can sub- stantially improve performances of drip irrigation. However, the findings are supported by the ESR framework (Hill, 1998) which emphases the upgrade of practices and efficiency increase as first of agroecological transition process. Finally, one can rightly wonder about the interest for a farmer to switch to an agroforestry system in return for a reduced yield. Hence the interest to evaluate the total profitability of the system. The main limit of this approach is that it does not analyze the gross margin, which better reflects the profitability. Neverthe- less, this exploratory analysis gives a concrete vision of the possibilities offered by agroecology to improve drip irrigation performance. [SOURCE: Page_155]

Chapter 6 [SOURCE: Page_158]

General discussion and conclusion [SOURCE: Page_158]

This final chapter aims at: [SOURCE: Page_158]

• analyzing the robustness of the conclusions, in methodological and result terms, by comparing the analyses provided by the different but comple- mentary approaches used in this research: the description of the diverse technical management routes applied to chili-pepper-based crop systems and their qualitative assessment (chapter 2), the quantitative assessment of the environmental impacts of these TMRs (chapter 4) and the analysis scenarios of improvement of practices (Chapter 5), [SOURCE: Page_158]

• discussing the global methodological approach, [SOURCE: Page_158]

• presenting the limits of the study and perspectives of future research ac- tions on the subject. [SOURCE: Page_158]

Starting from the postulate that, despite its limits, drip irrigation could be- come a lever of action towards the agroecological management of crops in the Southern Mediterranean context and more generally within Mediterranean or semi-arid climates, this thesis analyzes this technology in a broader perspective, considering it as a potential vector of interesting practices, that could contribute to initiate a transition towards more sustainable cropping systems. Table 6.1 summarizes the problematic and objectives of the thesis. The methodology used to treat each question and the key findings are synthesized on Figure 6.1. [SOURCE: Page_158]

6.1    Summary of results As the overall goal, this thesis aimed at analyzing whether locally existing agroe- cological practices can be a lever of action to improve the performances of drip irrigation technology. [SOURCE: Page_158]

120                               Chapter 6. General discussion and conclusion [SOURCE: Page_159]

Table 6.1: Research questions and objectives Questions How can agroecology be integrated into the understanding of irrigated systems using drip irrigation? Is the implementation of agroecological practices in these systems a so- lution to improve resource use efficiency and address environmental issues? General objective                     Specific objectives A thorough and methodic de- scription of the diversity of farm- ing technical management routes (TMRs) for a representative crop. Analyze the conditions in which lo- cally existing agroecological prac- An assessment of the environmen- tices, integrated within technical tal impacts of these diverse tech- management routes applied by nical management routes, using farmers, can be a lever to im- both qualitative and quantitative prove performances of drip irriga- approaches. tion technology. The analysis of improvement path- ways regarding irrigation perfor- mance, through the dual integra- tion of agroecological practices and new patterns of irrigation schedul- ing. [SOURCE: Page_159]

Through a thorough description of the diversity of farming technical man- agement routes (TMRs) on a sample of 45 plots, and their qualitative assessment, this work first showed that highly contrasted TMRs existed within the study zone, despite its relatively limited size and similarities in farmers’ technical and socio-economic conditions (chapter 2). Seven typical TMRs were found, with a gradient ranging from non-environmental-friendly to more environmental- friendly ones. This qualitative assessment of environmental impacts is however not straightforward as identified TMRs were constituted of various technical operations each integrating more or less sustainable practices. On the one hand, several practices presented in the literature as agroecology-compatible (Wezel et al., 2014; Ameur et al., 2020) were identified within these TMRs, including the use and conservation of local chili-pepper varieties, relay cropping, tree-annual crop associations, cucurbitaceae or leguminous, integrated crop-livestock sys- tems which enabled crop residue grazing or organic fertilization. On the other hand, the TMRs were combinations of these agroecology-compatible practices with less virtuous practices such as high level of pest management product ap- plication or high levels of fertilization. Hence, we demonstrate that the drip irrigation technology contributes to diversify farming practices, by enabling the [SOURCE: Page_159]

122                                 Chapter 6. General discussion and conclusion [SOURCE: Page_161]

• to what extent the agroecological practices characterized within the TMRs can be a solution to improve the current cropping systems? [SOURCE: Page_161]

Answers (synthesized in figure 6.1) are provided to these questions hereafter, focusing specifically on water and nitrogen resources. With the quantitative as- sessment undertaken on a limited number of 11 representative fields (chapters 3 and 4), we have shown that, in general, most farmers’ fertigation strategies generate massive drainage and nitrate leaching. However, some farmers’ strate- gies allow to achieve relatively low amounts of drainage and nitrate leaching. We also found that the management of fertigation could be improved to reduce impacts of water losses and limit pollutant transfer towards the aquifer. We evaluated this potential of improvement and found that substantial water and fertilizers savings were possible resulting in significant yield gain in most cases. In addition, a cross-analysis of findings of both qualitative and quantita- tive assessments enables to state that, in general, i) non-environmental-friendly TMRs led to high yield; inversely, ii) environmental-friendly TMRs generated lower yields; iii) some environmental-friendly TMRs, were as productive as non-environmental-friendly TMRs. Indeed, fields representing cluster 1 and 2 - non-environmental-friendly TMRs (Chapter 2) - were the most productive (29 to 66 T.ha 1 in marketable yield). Most environmental-friendly TMRs of cluster 6 were less productive (8 T.ha 1 maximum). Amidst these yields gen- erated by both (non)-environmental-friendly TMRs, intermediary TMRs have generated intermediary yields (10 to 33 T.ha 1 ). Nonetheless, P2 (cluster 5) and P10 (cluster 2), which unlike the other fields within the same category ((non)- environmental-friendly), generated respectively high and low yields. Hence, even though TMRs play a key role, yields are highly linked to the variety used, as expressed by farmers: “hybrid varieties are far more productive than local varieties.” So, the varietal choices play a crucial role in productivity, but the farmer does not always adapt his management to this parameter. The results tie well with previous works wherein yield was estimated to about 70 T.ha 1 for the hybrid variety and 12 T.ha 1 for the local variety (Morel 2018, unpub- lished report). Considering the whole TMR, despite some limits, partly related to non-optimum fertigation strategies, we found that there are TMRs adapted to both varieties, as illustrated by P2, allowing to relatively high productivities and efficiency (P2 presented the highest NUE Figure 6.2) Finally, we found that the performance of the studied systems can be sub- stantially improved through the dual integration of agroecological practices (ma- nure tea and agroforestry) and optimized patterns of irrigation scheduling. Al- though the adoption of these alternative solutions can generate additional in- come, they might also create additional production costs and workload depend- ing on cases. These improvements pathways should be adapted to each farmer’s [SOURCE: Page_161]

### 6.1. Summary of results                                                            123

Figure 6.2:     Synthesis of results and key variables and indicators describing TMRs. [SOURCE: Page_162]

6.1.1    Contribution of the thesis [SOURCE: Page_162]

The contribution of the thesis are threefold. A generic and conceptual contribu- tion lies in the overcoming of the apparent antagonism between agroecological practices and irrigated agriculture. Looking back at the problematic, objectives and recommendations for the research agenda concerning drip irrigation, pre- sented in Chapter 1, the thesis’ contribution to knowledge are declined in both findings and methodological approaches. [SOURCE: Page_162]

6.1.2    Contribution to knowledge [SOURCE: Page_162]

This thesis highlights four major points. [SOURCE: Page_162]

1. In real conditions, technical management routes applied to one crop sys- tem can be highly diverse. They are often a combination of diverse farm- ing practices, involving both agroecology-compatible and conventional (chemically intensive) practices. [SOURCE: Page_162]

2. Crop management is evidently conducted to lead to good economic re- turns, but the farmer’s profile plays a crucial role in the final applied man- agement rules. This is reflected in both the applied TMRs and variety choices, and the structure of the farm: some seeking to maximize profit on rental land, while others seek to combine economic profitability with soil conservation on land they own. [SOURCE: Page_162]

3. Under farmers’ conditions, and in most cases, irrigation scheduling of drip-irrigated market gardening crops (based on the analysis of chili-pepper cultivation practices) at the plot scale is highly inefficient. Substantial in- creases in efficiencies should be realized to achieve the high performances [SOURCE: Page_162]

124                                 Chapter 6. General discussion and conclusion [SOURCE: Page_163]

sought with the use of drip irrigation. Locally, there is an evident potential of improvement of irrigation performance, in both economic and environ- mental terms. [SOURCE: Page_163]

4. Improvement pathways of drip-irrigated market gardening systems can involve optimized irrigation scheduling, but could also be accomplished through the integration of agroecological practices. These technical and system-level improvements are complementary, in the sense that they im- pact different dimensions of sustainability (socio-economic, agronomic, environmental), thus contributing to improve both resource use and the economic interest. [SOURCE: Page_163]

Through our research approach involving directly farmers as suggested by Benouniche et al. (2014), this thesis suggests a possible compatibility between agroecological practices and irrigation. Yet, this co-existence is not straightfor- ward as the current paradigm is a tendency to make an antagonism between agroecology and irrigated agriculture (pollution, water wastage, soil saliniza- tion, biodiversity modification. . . ). Indeed, as shown in Chapter 1, from the point of view of irrigation science, improved efficiencies can be sought through better irrigation scheduling and technological innovation. In the studied case, this pathway can be limited by its effects on increased labor work. In opposition, agroecologist, emphasis agronomic improvements based ecological intensifica- tion trough recycling, substitution, diversity, etc. Thus, adopting other technical operations (based on these elements) and even adapting the cropping systems constitutes another option to better valorize the resources. In this thesis, we have shown that although these two methods of improvement of agricultural systems primarily appear antagonist, they can be complementary. We demon- strate the facilitation relation existing between the drip irrigation technology and the adoption of agroecology-compatible practices in the Merguellil down- stream plain. This constitutes a significant step forward in research on agroecol- ogy in irrigated systems. From an agronomic point of view, water management is very marginal in research on agroecology and is often limited to the sole improvement of the ef- ficiency of its use. The thesis treats water in irrigated systems from a different angle and shows how access to this resource, where it is a limiting factor, can catalyze the emergence of some agroecology-compatible practices. This analysis is conducted at the plot (sometimes to farm) and TMR levels, and does not im- ply sustainability at higher organization levels. It also invites to consider these new technologies as potential vectors of new practices, which can be more sus- tainable despite the threat of a rebound effect at a higher level. Returning to the ESR conceptual framework presented in figure 1.5, the the- sis brings a core set of knowledges concerning the first stage (Efficiency increase) [SOURCE: Page_163]

### 6.1. Summary of results                                                        125

and to some extend the second stage (substitution) in the Merguellil down- stream plain context. It emphasizes the already high efficiency increase poten- tial of these irrigated systems, demonstrated by the estimated excessive amount of water and nutrient used and modelled yield gain potentials. Higher pro- ductivity and efficiencies of resources use can be achieved on almost all fields, through optimized irrigation scheduling, or the use for example of manure tea and agroforestry. Concerning substitution, we have shown that there are sets of single practices, related to agroecology, that can be alternatives to conven- tional practices, that can serve to reduce resources’ overuse and wastage, reduce dependency to external input, while increasing productivity. Some are, in this Mediterranean context, specific to irrigated systems, such as organic fertigation or the possibility of integrating trees in the crop system. However, we also show that these innovations are drowned within a wide range of other management rules. The findings are valid for most vegetable crops, since farmers tend to have the same logics of management of vegetable crops as observed during the study. Furthermore, the findings enable us to support Benouniche et al. (2014)’s statement, that considers drip irrigation performances as a myth. The massive drainage and nitrate leaching revealed in our case-study site, confirmed else- where (Laib et al., 2018), show that it is not enough to simply adopt the drip irrigation technology, but more importantly, strategies of fertigation must be up- graded and adapted to the technology. Hence, the results question the position of some authors such as Wezel (2017), presenting drip irrigation as an agroe- cological practice of water saving to reach efficiencies sought by agroecology. Rather, our results suggest that drip irrigation might be considered as the vec- tor of some environmental-friendly practices, and if managed in the right way, it offers the potential to increase water and nutrient use efficiencies and crop productivity. [SOURCE: Page_164]

6.1.3   Methodological contribution [SOURCE: Page_164]

On the methodological plan, the thesis deals with one of the main challenges of research in irrigation water management: the multidisciplinarity. As men- tioned in Chapter 1, each discipline spanned by the subject of irrigation has, so far, the tendency to focus on its own specialty using specific tools (Hsiao et al., 2007). The core findings of this thesis have been obtained using a set of comple- mentary analysis frameworks and tools coming from different disciplines, from typology (agronomy of practices) to qualitative assessment of environmental impacts (hydrology, ecology), and field monitoring and modelling (agronomy, agro-hydrology). Such an approach has not been implemented before, especially concerning questions related to agroecology in irrigated systems. A similar com- bination of approaches has been implemented to analyze agroforestry systems [SOURCE: Page_164]

126                                  Chapter 6. General discussion and conclusion [SOURCE: Page_165]

in rainfed conditions in Morocco (Temani et al., 2021). In addition, we designed and used methods to implement all these frameworks in highly constraining field conditions, focusing on farmers’ practices. Such an environment is partic- ularly unfavorable to in-depth analyses and approaches, as those implemented here. Beyond the complementarity of analysis frameworks, the originality of the thesis also lies in the use of different but complementary methods of data col- lection and analysis, from surveys to field measurements and observation or simulation. The diagnostic analysis primarily realized through a typology anal- ysis was undertaken in order to stay in line with one the fundamental principles of agroecology suggesting an understanding of local farming practices and con- ditions before proposing adaptation solutions. The choice to start by a typology analysis (Figure 6.1) was also dictated by the lack of documentation on TMRs in the Merguellil downstream basin since the widespread adoption of drip ir- rigation. The typology represents the basis of the study, and made it possible, although only partly, to meet several objectives of the Ph.D. Thus, in this step, we i) thoroughly described the diversity of TMRs, ii) assessed them, iii) identi- fied some agroecology-compatible practices and iv) suggested some alternative management rules exclusively based on local practices. Beyond the necessity to understand how farmers operate, to propose adapted solutions, field monitor- ing was preferred to controlled experimentation for several reasons: [SOURCE: Page_165]

• the representation of peasant practices in controlled environments is often biased, [SOURCE: Page_165]

• only well-studied and documented practices can be replicated in a con- trolled environment, [SOURCE: Page_165]

• involving farmers in the experimentation increases the chances that the alternative solution we propose will be suitable for them, [SOURCE: Page_165]

This choice has allowed us to identify the perverse effects of drip irrigation – reflected by a weak appropriation of fertigation management – hitherto hidden by the theoretical performance of this technology. Further, we used a modelling approach based on these field data collected in real environment, which was a real challenge. The use of a modelling approach to represent farmers’ fertiga- tion strategies had a twofold advantage. On the one hand, it combined, within a single conceptual framework, very disparate information (agricultural prac- tices, abiotic constraints) to understand plant’s responses. On the other hand, it allowed to estimate variables (drainage, nitrate leaching) without going through costly and time-consuming measurements, and to explore the potential effect of an upgrade of farmers’ practices without additional experimentation. Despite [SOURCE: Page_165]

### 6.2. Limits of the study                                                        127

numerous sources of uncertainty related to data, modelling gave coherent ex- planations of the impacts of farmers’ TMRs on the studied systems’ externalities (yield, drainage, nitrate leaching). The analysis of optimization scenarios, al- though exploratory, is a way to support our analyses with numerical data. This exploratory analysis, based on simplistic but realistic hypotheses, enabled to confirm the interest of looking at an access to water resources and technology rather as an opportunity than as an obstacle for the transition to more economi- cally and environmentally viable agricultural systems. [SOURCE: Page_166]

6.2     Limits of the study 6.2.1   The scale of analysis and studied systems [SOURCE: Page_166]

In hindsight, this thesis was mainly focused on the efficiency increase stage of the incremental phase of ESR framework. This stage mainly concerns field and farm scales. We have shown that there is a high potential to increase efficiencies of resource use. The levels 2 (substitution) and 3 (Redesign) of the ESR con- ceptual framework should be studied more thoroughly. Indeed, although we analyzed the interest of the substitution of fertilizers with manure tea and the re- design of the systems into agroforestry systems, the assumptions we made were simplistic and conclusions are exploratory. The strong and widespread adop- tion of these practices within the plain shows an evident, potentially economic interest of farmers in them. One of the main goals sought by farmers, as shown by Ameur et al. (2020), is economic profitability. Conclusions that emerged from our economic analysis must be taken with caution. Indeed, price volatility can actually make less productive systems more economically viable as price may vary by a factor of two. We suggest that the economic interest and ecosys- tem services provided by the alternative practices have to be more assessed in further studies. Furthermore, as suggested by Duru et al. (2015) and Rund- gren (2015), the evolution of irrigated systems can get stuck in unsustainable states if the changes implemented are solely incremental ("Efficiency increase" or "Substitution"). Our works are complementary with Ameur et al. (2020)’s studies and both represent springboard towards redesign practices, based on local knowledge. However, a limit of the thesis was the lack of detailed field data to support the analysis of agroecological scenarios. However, important efforts have been made to monitor agroforestry system. The experimentation was not as satisfactory as expected. As possible perspectives of research at plot level, the agroecology-compatible practices described in this study should be further studies, seeking what logics affect the adoption of these practices. What biophysical processes are affected at plant scale? At territorial scale, one needs to perform assessment of the level of adaptation of the agroecology-compatible [SOURCE: Page_166]

128                                   Chapter 6. General discussion and conclusion [SOURCE: Page_167]

practices. Based on these practices, co-design of alternative systems could be realized leading to substitution and redesign phase. [SOURCE: Page_167]

6.2.2   A farmer and field-focused analysis [SOURCE: Page_167]

Our methodological approach, although centered on the farmer, was not im- plemented in a participatory way. Thus, the contributions of this thesis (typ- ical TMRs, alternative practices such as optimized irrigation scheduling, etc.) would require a validation involving farmers and local stakeholders. In agree- ment with Levard et al. (2019), this consultation on the results and the poten- tial effects of the adoption of alternative practices is essential. To overcome the weakness of the thesis, a restitution / validation workshop, gathering the con- cerned stakeholders could have been organized 1 . This would be an opportunity to discuss whether new cropping systems can be designed from the proposed TMRs. It could also allow to verify in what extent farmers might be willing to adopt the alternative practices suggested by the thesis. For instance, we are aware that our optimized irrigation scheduling can be too binding for farmers, as these recommend more frequent irrigation and “shorter” irrigation. Some trade-off frequency-dose (duration) could be co-elaborated in a participative ap- proach. Pushing further, a participatory implementation of our methodology is also possible. For instance, one could realize farmer and expert-based typol- ogy and assessment of TMRs. Such an approach would have the advantage to allow a co-description/evaluation of the existing management rules and a co- conception of alternative solution. Indeed, the development of an agriculture enhancing ecosystem services, involves co-evolution of technological, social, economic and even institutional dimensions, and lies on appropriate interac- tions between stake-holders of the farming system, the supply chain and natural resources management at local scale (Duru et al., 2015). These interactions are not explicitly taken into account in the thesis. Audouin et al. (2019) support the importance of the co-evaluation and the necessity to have a multi-stakeholder representation in the approaches. Some results coming from a participatory im- plementation of our methodology might probably differ from ours. Different scenarios might also be chosen. However, at this stage of understanding, knowl- edge stemming from the numerous interviews with stakeholders – although not explicitly described and analyzed – suggests that the gradient of environmental- friendly practices is also perceived as such by farmers and should emerge from participatory implementation of our methodology. [SOURCE: Page_167]

1 This step was planned but not implemented, due to Covid-19 pandemic restrictions. [SOURCE: Page_167]

### 6.2. Limits of the study                                                       129

6.2.3   Comparison of systems and criteria used [SOURCE: Page_168]

The monitored plots in 2019 were close to the cluster paragons defined in the ty- pology analysis (chapter 2), hence giving for one crop cycle, data on the resulting yields and water and fertilizer use. This completes the initial, more qualitative analysis concerning these main variables. In the following assessments, fields were compared to each other. This allowed to compare for each efficiency indi- cator we computed, the fields relatively to the best performing field. No refer- ence situation was thus considered. According to Levard et al. (2019), it is an ap- parent limit of the study. Indeed, the study should be based on the comparison between field or farms where certain agroecological practices and systems are implemented and "reference" fields or farms where these are not implemented. In fact, it is more complex to identify a reference situation when the study in- volves existing practices, even the more when a large variety of practices are implemented in a wide variety of production systems. A reference field would be necessary to test newly designed cropping systems or management rules. Further, the quantitative assessment realized in the thesis, based on the in- dicators traditionally used, gives it a high degree of generality. The main limi- tation lies in the dimension and criteria assessed. Indeed, the thesis focused on agro-environmental aspects and especially productivity and the resources use efficiencies. Additional dimension should have been integrated (the effects of greenhouse gas emissions, the effectiveness of pest and disease control, socio- economic performances). More specifically, soil health especially soil biologi- cal activity, although initially planned, was not included in the study due to the 2020 lockdown. Soil is a key component of agricultural ecosystems and might not be occlude in agroecological assessment of farming systems. Indica- tor such as basal respiration may have provided additional interesting insights to the study. We initially planned to compare at least both environmental and non-environmental friendly TMRs regarding basal respiration. Administrative shutdowns did not allow us to realize the laboratory analyses. Hence, further studies might investigate these aspects. For instance, a multicriterial assessment of TMRs could be undertaken, in order to broaden the dimension and scale of analysis. Moreover, a limit of the quantitative assessment was the low number of fields per cluster to compare the results. The fact that only one field repre- sented cluster 3, 4 and 5 for example, did not allow to assess the intra-cluster variability of performances. Further research might investigate this intra-cluster productivity and eventually compare local and hybrid varieties within clusters. [SOURCE: Page_168]

6.2.4   Two-year complementary analyses [SOURCE: Page_168]

Multiple complementary approaches have been used to describe and assess the TMRs. Nonetheless, these approaches were applied on different cropping years [SOURCE: Page_168]

130                                    Chapter 6. General discussion and conclusion [SOURCE: Page_169]

(2018, 2019). To do so, we assumed that farmers’ strategies might not change substantially over time. Comparison of some data collected both years revealed there were no significant changes in farmers’ strategies (e.g. moving from environmental- friendly to non-environmental friendly). However, this hypothesis can be called into question as some of the agro- nomic and environmental performance achieved in 2019 can be (partly) attributed to changes that occurred between these two periods, particularly in relation to factors such as climate, economic conditions and different social conditions. As illustration, some farmers said during the surveys in 2019: [SOURCE: Page_169]

• "I am often out of the farm this year. I am supplying less fertilizers" (P$_3$) [SOURCE: Page_169]

• "I am not expecting a high yield this year due to weed attacks, I am not going to invest in this field anymore" (P$_7$) [SOURCE: Page_169]

• Another farmer decided not to apply organic manure in 2019; we sus- pected economic constraints [SOURCE: Page_169]

Thus, it would be interesting to implement the approaches in both 2018 and 2019 cropping years. However, proceeding that way would have generated ad- ditional methodological questions: which fields to monitor at the beginning of the study? how to justify the choice of these fields? Which data to collect? How to collect these data? As shown in figure. . . , we intentionally realized the typol- ogy and qualitative assessment in 2018, then the quantitative assessment the following year as further actions, based on the Bos et al.’ (2004) conceptual framework. [SOURCE: Page_169]

6.3      Perspectives 6.3.1     Agroecological perspectives in the Southern Mediterranean irri- gated systems [SOURCE: Page_169]

Several authors have described the trajectory of the irrigated agriculture in the Southern Mediterranean region. Massuel et al. (2017) showed the high amounts of water withdrawn at farm level each year in the Saiss plain in Morocco, the Merguellil downstream plain in Tunisia and the Ziban in Algeria. Venot et al. (2017) associated these withdrawals to a rebound effect of the adaption of the drip irrigation technology. Pradeleix et al. (2018) for their part, highlighted en- ergy used for groundwater pumping as one of the main processes contributing to the environmental impacts of farming practices on natural resources a terri- torial scale in the Merguellil downstream plain. Our study does support these overviews and shows the importance of the withdrawals, their impacts at plot scale and the necessity to improve the efficiencies. According to Pradeleix et [SOURCE: Page_169]

### 6.3. Perspectives                                                             131

al. (2018), fertilization is the most important factor contributing to environmen- tal impacts of farming practices. These findings confirm our results and are supported by other studies (Laib et al., 2018). Furthermore, this thesis support the idea that innovations cannot solely achieve the purpose of sustainable de- velopment in the agriculture sector in the Maghreb countries (Khaled, 2017). There is still a long way to go to achieve the transformative phases of the agroe- cological transition of the production systems in Tunisia as shown in this the- sis, in Morroco (Chbika and Aouane, 2021) and in the Maghreb countries in general. Agroecology-compatible practices and more generally environmental- friendly management which are remnants of ancient less-performing systems are doomed to gradually disappear in these regions. Indeed, considering the dynamics of changes in the production systems described by Azizi (2018) in the Merguellil downstream plain context, farmers tend to move towards more high value-added crops (market gardening and orchards) and land intensification with more productive TMRs. In such global intensification context, environmental- friendly TMRs will inevitably become less present. Hence, actions in the short and medium terms should involve the Efficiency increase and Substitution phases of the in agroecological transition process in the Southern Mediterranean irri- gated systems and sought to preserve the environmental-friendly TMRs. [SOURCE: Page_170]

6.3.2   Towards a territorial evaluation of irrigated market gardening crops [SOURCE: Page_170]

The approach implemented in this thesis is adaptable and applicable to other crops and other territories in order to assess the conditions of a transition to- wards more sustainable systems. The study can contribute to a broader field of research on regional sustainability of cropping practices. Indeed, the methodol- ogy implemented in chapter 1 defined the key TOs related to TMRs. However, the regional representativity of these TMRs must be studied. It could be more easily obtained by carrying out surveys on the key variables. This would al- low to identify new specificities of productions, changes in the link farm-TMR types, management rules and new innovative practices, to be taken into account for the design of agricultural policies. On another hand, the method could also be applied to other emblematic crops to test the consistency of our conclusions. It would be interesting, for example, to apply the approach to the case tomato or watermelon-based systems in the Merguellil plain, or in other country of the Southern Mediterranean, the onion-based systems of the Saiss plain in Morocco. As suggested by Duru et al. (2015) and Rundgren (2015), the evolution of irri- gated systems can get stuck in unsustainable states if the changes implemented are solely incremental ("Efficiency increase" or "Substitution"). Our works are [SOURCE: Page_170]

132                                Chapter 6. General discussion and conclusion [SOURCE: Page_171]

complementary with Ameur et al. (2020)’s studies and both represent spring- board towards redesign practices, based on local knowledge. However, a limit of the thesis was the lack of detailed field data to support the analysis of agroeco- logical scenarios. Although important efforts have been made to monitor agro- forestry system, the experimentation was not as satisfactory as expected. As possible perspectives of research at plot scale, the agroecology-compatible prac- tices described in this study should be further studied. What are the real cost related to these? What biophysical processes are affected at plant scale? Then, at territorial scale, one needs to perform assessment of the level of adaptation of the agroecology-compatible practices. Based on these practices, co-design of alternative systems could realized leading to substitution and redesign phase. [SOURCE: Page_171]

6.3.3   Socio-economic factors influencing management logics [SOURCE: Page_171]

To broaden the thoughts, a more global analysis could to be carried out at the farm and even territorial scales. In chapter 2, we identified the key structural variables allowing to identify which farm implemented each type of TMR. Be- yond the structural data, which logics drive the operational choices of farmers? We hypothesized that strong social, economic and environmental constraints in irrigated areas (Jamin et al., 2011) might enable a diversity of farming practices, and showed that environmental-friendly systems arose from this local diver- sity. We also showed that facilitated access to water resource and drip irriga- tion are not systemically an obstacle for the adoption of more environmental- friendly farming practices. In what extent social, economic and environmental constraints contribute to this adoption? What are the farmers’ decision rules in front of these constraints? Further, as major contribution, the thesis reassesses the place given to water and irrigation in agroecology. Would the assertion that agroecology and irrigation are compatible be valid in any irrigated system? [SOURCE: Page_171]

6.4     General conclusion Irrigated agriculture meets numerous challenges worldwide and more specif- ically in the southern Mediterranean where irrigation is instrumental for a vi- able agriculture, but where water is often managed in an unsustainable way. Although the principles of agroecology and those of irrigated agriculture are often considered to be contradictory, agroecology can actually be perceived as an alternative solution to deal with the challenges of irrigated systems based on drip irrigation technology. This thesis analyzed this technology as a potential vector of interesting practices to initiate a transition towards more sustainable systems. The general problematic of the study was: how can the multidimen- sional concept of agroecology be integrated into the understanding of irrigated [SOURCE: Page_171]

### 6.4. General conclusion                                                          133

systems using drip irrigation? Is the implementation of agroecological prac- tices in these systems a solution to resource use efficiency and environmental is- sues? Answers are brought to these questions by analyzing access to water and the use of drip irrigation in a broader perspective using different approaches. Thus, the thesis reassesses the paradigm tending to make an antagonism be- tween agroecological principles and irrigation by showing possibilities of co- existence of agroecological practices and irrigation within the Merguellil down- stream plain, a relatively small territory where farmers are confronted the same constraints. This was done by identifying sets environmental-friendly technical management rules (TMRs) within a large diversity of TMRs. TMRs were ac- cessed in both qualitative and quantitative manners. The findings showed that, although farmers did not design their TMRs in an agroecological perspective, they usually combine single agroecological practices to less virtuous practices to design more or less productive systems. Some TMRs adaptable to different crop- ping systems allow farmers to reach high productivities and efficiencies. Scien- tifically, this PhD uses approaches and tools from several disciplines including agronomy, hydrology and more specifically irrigation sciences. A set of statis- tical tools were combined to perform the typology analysis. Opitirrig, a model combining a crop model and water and nitrogen balances model was used to represent, in a unique conceptual framework, the farmers’ strategies and the pedoclimatic conditions. The use of this model enabled to project the possible resource savings and yield gain related to an eventual optimization of the farm- ers’ irrigation strategies. An integrative analysis of optimization strategies of the cropping systems was performed by applying both the generated optimization of irrigation scheduling and the integration of two representative agroecologi- cal practices observed in the region. These widespread practices might enable farmers to achieve economic goals or higher agro-environmental performances. This study raises further questions concerning the agroecological transition of irrigated systems. In a next step, a global analysis at the farm scale could be realized to understand how TMRs are arranged within cropping systems. [SOURCE: Page_172]

2. Agroecology in a semi-arid Mediterranean area: from real practices to integrated agroecological systems? Akakpo Koladé, Ameur Fatah, Bouarfa Sami, Leauthaud Crystèle. 2019. European Association of Agroecology. Herak- lion : European Association of Agroecology, Résumé, 1 p. Agroecology Europe Forum. 2, Heraklion, Grèce, 26 Septembre 2020/28 Septembre 2020. [SOURCE: Page_230]

Agroecology in a semi-arid Mediterranean area: from real practices to integrated agroecological systems? [SOURCE: Page_231]

The challenge of agriculture in the up-coming decades is to feed an ever-increasing population while reducing or getting rid of chemical inputs and preserving biodiversity and natural resources. In arid and semi-arid areas of the Mediterranean basin, farmers have to face this challenge, alongside multiple environmental and climatic risks. Due to low rainfall, irrigation is instrumental for agriculture and consequently for economy, in these areas. So far, irrigation has been analyzed in terms of eco-efficiency, and is commonly associated with intensification and pollution. First, this study aimed to identify, in a semi-arid irrigated area, a set of farming practices, already implemented by farmers, with agroecological potentials. We present in this communication two “virtuous” farming systems, compared to more conventional and intensive systems. Second, based on the results from 65 semi-structured interviews, a hypothetical plot with a more agroecological management style was designed. The study was carried out in the Merguellil irrigated plain in Central Tunisia, which is entirely cultivated, with a wide variety of crop types such as horticulture, fruit trees and cereals. A first farmer installed at the plot scale, a relay cropping of fava bean (Vicia faba) with pepper (Capsicum annuum) in intercropping with olive trees (Olea europaea). In this agroforestry system, the plot was further surrounded by a windbreak cereal (sorghum) belt. The farmer used old and local varieties, very resistant to pest attacks and that require low chemical inputs, resulting in lower cost of production. Seeds were saved and reused. At the farm level, crop residues (pepper, fava bean, olive tree pruning residue) and cereals were used for livestock feeding. Another farmer used manure as an organic fertilizer after a fermentation process. This practice lead to a substantial reduction of chemical nitrogen fertilizer use. In addition, he applied a water stress period at the beginning of the cycle, in order to “stimulate root growth and improve the plant's absorption capacity”. This allowed him to avoid the use of a root growth accelerator. In both cases, different types of spatial crop rotations and successions were set up with frequent fallow periods in order to restore soil fertility. However, these interesting practices coexist with other less virtuous practices. For example, most farmers continued to intensively apply pesticides and used very high quantities of chemical fertilizers. These farmers were mostly specialized in market garden and fruit trees, extremely water consuming. They generally used high yielding varieties, less resistant to pest attacks. Furthermore, the study showed that farmers usually combined agroecological practices for different purposes: i.e. income increase, soil fertility improvement, land-use optimization. All the practices mentioned above could be combined on a single plot/farm, giving a hypothetical system with a more agroecological orientation. With an adapted water and fertilization management, such a system could improve water and fertilizer efficiency and maintain soil quality. But this may not necessarily lead to better pest control or better yields. These practices remain to be evaluated and would be adopted by farmers only if they see it as a clear interest. [SOURCE: Page_231]

Appendix E. Conference abstracts                                         173 [SOURCE: Page_232]

3. Irrigation and fertigation practices in small-scale family farming sys- tems for water and nutrient efficiency in semi-arid Mediterranean areas. Akakpo Koladé, Leauthaud Crystèle, Bouarfa Sami. 2019. IRSTEA. Montpellier : IRSTEA, Résumé, 1 p. Water Savings in Irrigation, Montpellier, France, 13 Novembre 2019/14 Novembre 2019. https://watersaving.sciencesconf.org/289940 [SOURCE: Page_232]

Irrigation and fertigation practices in small-scale family farming systems for water and nutrient efficiency in semi-arid Mediterranean areas [SOURCE: Page_233]

Kolade Akakpo 1 , Crystele Leauthaud†2 , and Sami Bouarfa‡3 1 Gestion de l’Eau, Acteurs et Usages (UMR G-EAU) – Montpellier SupAgro – 361 rue J.F. Breton - BP 5095 34196 Montpellier Cedex 5, France 2 Gestion de l’Eau, Acteurs et Usages (UMR G-EAU) – Centre de coopération internationale en recherche agronomique pour le développement [CIRAD] – 361 rue J.F. Breton - BP 5095 34196 Montpellier Cedex 5, France 3 Gestion de l’Eau, Acteurs et Usages (UMR G-EAU) – Irstea – 361 rue J.F. Breton - BP 5095 34196 Montpellier Cedex 5, France [SOURCE: Page_233]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_233]

Efficient use of water and nutrients in irrigation system is important in arid and semi- arid regions, where water is a limiting resource. This study aims to analyze irrigation and fertigation practices for a widespread crop (pepper, Capsicum annuum) in Tunisia, in a context of highly diverse agricultural practices. The study took place in the irrigated plain of the Merguellil river in Central Tunisia, characterized by its high diversity of crops and practices. In 2019, irrigation and fertilization practices involved in pepper crop cultivation watered by drip irrigation, were compared on fourteen plots. This was carried out through an approach coupling semi-directive surveys with farmers and monitoring of irrigation, done using a temperature logger installed on the irrigation pipes and allowing the detection of irrigation duration. The study covered the three main seasons of pepper cultivation (Spring, Summer and late Summer). The results showed globally an opposition of two main logics. On the one hand, there are strategies aiming to a significant water supply during the first days after planting, followed by a non-irrigated period, forcing the plant to ”tap into the soil water reserves”, then irrigation at regular rates (once every three to five days or more) thereafter, sometime alternate with fertigation. On the other hand, there are strategies of regular water and nutrients supply, taking into account crop requirements: low water inputs at the beginning (on daily or once every two to four days rate), followed by a progressive increase. In this strategy, application of fertilizers through the drip irrigation occurred right after the first irrigations, the farmer alternating up to three irrigations for one fertigation. Commonly, fertilization was fractioned and application was done in the last quarter of watering time in order to avoid/reduce nutrient leaching. The number of splits varied significantly from a farmer to another and from a strategy to another. These irrigation and fertigation practices may have different effects on the system outputs, mainly on water and nitrogen efficiency and yield. Speaker † Corresponding author: [EMAIL_REDACTED] ‡ Corresponding author: [EMAIL_REDACTED] [SOURCE: Page_233]
